# 1. RESPIRATORY MECHANICS

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|Part A: Respiratory System]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|Part B: C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: Medical Knowledge]]

## 1.1. EQUATION OF MOTION
**The equation of motion describes the pressures needed to overcome elastic and resistive forces during ventilation.**

### 1.1.1. BASIC PRINCIPLES OF AIRFLOW

* Air moves into lungs due to pressure difference between mouth and alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* During unassisted breathing, diaphragm movement creates negative pleural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* During controlled breathing, positive pressure comes from mechanical ventilator [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.1.2. MAJOR IMPEDIMENTS TO AIRFLOW

* Airway/tissue resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Elastance (stiffness) of respiratory system [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.1.3. MATHEMATICAL DESCRIPTION

* Total airway pressure (Paw) can be expressed as:
   * P_aw = P_res + P_el + PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * P_aw = P_vent + P_musc = V_t × E_RC + R_aw × V̇_I + PEEP + PEEP_i [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * *Pvent = (Vt × ERS) + (Raw × V̇I) + PEEP + PEEPi - Pmus* [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Where:
   * Pvent: ventilator airway pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Pmus: pressure from patient inspiratory muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Vt: tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * ERS: respiratory system elastance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Raw: airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * V̇I: inspiratory flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * PEEP: positive end-expiratory pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * PEEPi: intrinsic PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The equation of motion mathematically describes how ventilator pressure, lung elasticity, airway resistance, and PEEP interact to determine the total pressure in the airways during mechanical ventilation.

## 1.2. WORK AND POWER OF BREATHING
**The energy required to overcome elastic and resistive forces during breathing.**

### 1.2.1. WORK OF BREATHING

* Expressed in joules per liter of ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 1J = energy needed for 1L gas across 10cmH2O pressure difference [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Higher pressure requires more work [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.2.2. MECHANICAL POWER

* Work applied to lung per unit time (joules/minute) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ Determines lung injury during mechanical ventilation via energy transfer to tissues [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.2.3. CLINICAL GOALS

* Reduce work of breathing while minimizing mechanical power applied to lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Balance between patient effort and ventilator support [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Work of breathing quantifies the energy expenditure during respiration, while mechanical power accounts for the rate of energy delivery, which can directly impact ventilator-induced lung injury.

## 1.3. ELASTIC FORCES
**Forces that resist lung inflation and promote return to resting position.**

### 1.3.1. COMPLIANCE

* **Definition**: Change in volume per unit change in transmural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Total respiratory system compliance (CRS) includes:
   * Lung compliance (CL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Chest wall compliance (CCW) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In healthy individuals: CL/CRS ratio ≈ 0.5 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* CRS is nonlinear across lung volumes:
   * Peak compliance occurs near functional residual capacity (FRC) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * As lung volume increases/decreases beyond FRC, compliance worsens [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Relationship between compliances: 1/C_L + 1/C_cw = 1/C_RS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.3.2. TRANSMURAL (TRANSPULMONARY) PRESSURE

* **Definition**: Difference between alveolar pressure (Palv) and pleural pressure (Ppl) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Palv estimated by airway pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Ppl estimated by esophageal pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PL is marker of lung stress (force applied over lung area) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* For chest wall, PL = atmospheric pressure - Ppl [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image.png_9b0598.png_07c048.pn_.png)

### 1.3.3. SURFACE TENSION AND SURFACTANT

* Lung architecture (bronchioles and alveolar walls tethered together) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Surface tension tends to collapse alveoli as size decreases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Surfactant mitigates surface tension effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Laplace equation**: P = 2T/R [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * P: pressure within bubble (dyn × cm⁻²) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * T: surface tension (dyn × cm⁻¹) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * R: bubble radius (cm) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Surface tension dynamics create hysteresis (different pressure-volume relationships during inspiration vs expiration) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![[Pasted image 20250522201608.png]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_1.png_d1a333.png_a5da0d._.png)

**Bottom Line:** Elastic forces in the respiratory system arise from tissue elasticity and surface tension, with compliance describing how easily the lungs and chest wall expand. Transmural pressure gradients drive volume changes, while surfactant reduces the tendency of alveoli to collapse.

## 1.4. BRONCHOPULMONARY SEGMENTS
**Anatomical divisions of the lungs defined by branching of the bronchial tree.**

| **Lung Side/Lobe** | **Bronchopulmonary Segment**                                                                                               |     |
| ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
| *Right Upper*      | Apical, Anterior, Posterior [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System                                            | ⓘ]] |
| *Right Middle*     | Medial, Lateral [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System                                                        | ⓘ]] |
| *Right Lower*      | Superior, Medial basal, Lateral basal, Anterior basal, Posterior basal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System | ⓘ]] |
| *Left Upper*       | Apical posterior, Anterior [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System                                             | ⓘ]] |
| *Left Lingula*     | Superior, Inferior [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System                                                     | ⓘ]] |
| *Left Lower*       | Superior, Posterior basal, Anteromedial basal, Lateral basal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System           | ⓘ]] |

**Bottom Line:** Understanding bronchopulmonary segments is essential for localizing pathology, interpreting imaging, and planning procedures such as bronchoscopy or lung resection.

## 1.5. MEASURING ELASTIC FORCES DURING ANESTHESIA
**Methods to assess lung and chest wall mechanics during anesthesia and mechanical ventilation.**

### 1.5.1. IDEAL MEASUREMENT

* Should be done during prolonged inspiratory pause (>3s) at plateau pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.5.2. PRACTICAL CHALLENGES

* True absence of flow never occurs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Gas redistribution occurs between lung compartments (*pendelluft*) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal lung has minimal pendelluft due to homogeneous time constants [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.5.3. CLINICAL APPROXIMATION WITH VENTILATORS

* Brief end-inspiratory pause (0.2-1s) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* CRS = ΔVt/ΔP where ΔP = Plateau - PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Flow still present resulting in higher pressures and lower calculated compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** While theoretically elastic forces should be measured during complete airflow cessation, practical clinical measurements use brief inspiratory holds, recognizing limitations from ongoing gas redistribution.

## 1.6. DRIVING PRESSURE
**A key parameter for monitoring mechanical ventilation that better reflects lung stress than tidal volume alone.**

### 1.6.1. DEFINITION

* Plateau pressure - PEEP; or Vt/respiratory system compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.6.2. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

* Surrogate for global lung strain [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Marker of ventilator-induced injury [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Predictor of mortality in ARDS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Accounts for pre-existing lung characteristics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **HY**: Lower driving pressures correlate with lower postoperative pulmonary complications (PPC) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 1.6.3. TIDAL VOLUME CONSIDERATIONS

* With low driving pressure/strain, no need to reduce Vt to meet arbitrary weight-based guidelines [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Doing so may cause atelectasis and ventilator desynchrony in non-paralyzed patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Lowering Vt (≤6ml/kg) may increase mortality when CRS is high (as seen in ARDS) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Driving pressure provides a more individualized approach to mechanical ventilation than tidal volume alone, reflecting the actual mechanical stress applied to the lungs and correlating with clinical outcomes.

## 1.7. ESOPHAGEAL PRESSURE AND CHEST WALL COMPLIANCE
**Using esophageal pressure monitoring to distinguish between lung and chest wall mechanics.**

### 1.7.1. PURPOSE

* Allows separate determination of CL and CCw [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* C_L = \Delta V_t/[P_L, end-inspiration - P_L, end-expiration] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* C_cw = \Delta V_t/[P_pl, end-inspiration - P_pl, end-expiration] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.7.2. TECHNIQUE

* Esophageal pressure (Pes) is surrogate for pleural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Insertion of balloon catheter filled with air into esophagus, connected to pressure transducer [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.7.3. PRESSURE COMPONENTS

* Total pressure needed for inflation (ΔPaw) divided into:
   * Pressure moving lungs (ΔPL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Pressure moving chest wall (ΔPpl) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Esophageal pressure monitoring enables differentiation between lung and chest wall mechanics, particularly valuable in conditions affecting chest wall compliance (obesity, pneumoperitoneum) to guide optimal ventilator settings.

## 1.8. CHANGES IN ELASTIC FORCES UNDER ANESTHESIA
**How anesthesia and surgical positioning affect lung mechanics.**

### 1.8.1. FUNDAMENTAL CHANGES

* Lungs become smaller and relatively stiffer [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Atelectasis is universal in anesthetized patients and may persist well into recovery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 1.8.2. ATELECTASIS MECHANISMS

* **Compressive forces** from increased regional pleural pressure (gravity-dependent) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Resorption**: gas flow out of alveoli > gas flow in (as distal airways close) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Exacerbated by high pleural pressures (obese patients under GA in Trendelenburg) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * May occur with low V/Q ratios or high FiO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.8.3. PLEURAL PRESSURE CHANGES

* Increased during anesthesia as diaphragm shifts into thorax [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Minimized with upright positioning [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Increases with Trendelenburg, obesity, or inappropriately low Vt [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Loss of respiratory muscle tone decreases chest wall cross-sectional area [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 1.8.4. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* High airway pressure needed to maintain positive transpulmonary pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Can apply airway pressure without excessive lung stress or hemodynamic compromise [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: PROBESE trial showed higher PEEP (12cmH2O) with recruitment maneuvers improved intraoperative oxygenation but didn't reduce PPCs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 1.8.5. RECRUITMENT TECHNIQUE EXAMPLE

* PCV with driving pressure 10cmH2O [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* I:E ratio 1:1, RR 10 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Stepwise PEEP increase by 5cmH2O every 30 seconds [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Maximum plateau pressure 50cmH2O for 1 minute [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 1.8.6. EFFECTS OF OBESITY AND PNEUMOPERITONEUM

* Obesity shifts pressure-volume curve of chest wall right without changing shape [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Pneumoperitoneum reduces chest wall compliance but preserves lung compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Higher proportion of airway pressure goes to moving chest wall rather than lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Anesthesia universally causes atelectasis through compression and resorption mechanisms, requiring higher airway pressures to maintain lung inflation, especially in obese patients or those in Trendelenburg position.

# 2. RESISTIVE FORCES


## 2.1. MAJOR COMPONENTS
**The primary sources of resistance in the respiratory system.**

* **Gas flow resistance** in airways: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Upper airways, trachea, bronchi, ETT [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Resistance to lung/chest wall tissue movement** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.1.1. EFFECTS OF HIGH RESISTANCE

* Elevated airway pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Lengthened expiratory time constant (lung emptying time) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.1.2. EXPIRATORY RESISTANCE

* Mild resistance overcome through augmented inspiratory work [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Excessive resistance leads to incomplete expiration, hyperinflation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Requires accessory muscles to expel air (not possible under GA or if paralyzed) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Resistive forces in the respiratory system come from both airway resistance to gas flow and tissue resistance to movement, affecting the work of breathing and potentially leading to hyperinflation if excessive.

## 2.2. AIRWAY ANATOMY (ADULT)
**Structure of the airways relevant to anesthesia practice.**

### 2.2.1. TRACHEA

* 10-12cm long, 20mm diameter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* U-shaped hyaline cartilage support (opening facing posteriorly) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Cricoid membrane tethers trachea to cricoid cartilage at C6 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Half intrathoracic, half extrathoracic [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Bifurcates at sternal angle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Adult carina can move up to 5cm from normal position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.2. ETT POSITIONING

* 🔑 **HY**: ETT distal end moves average 3.8cm with neck flexion/extension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Neck flexion moves ETT toward carina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Neck extension moves ETT away from carina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* More critical in infants/children where small movements can cause mainstem intubation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.3. MAINSTEM BRONCHI

* Right bronchus generally wider than left [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Right bronchus leaves trachea at ~25° angle, left at ~45° angle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Right mainstem at higher risk for endobronchial intubation or aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* In children <3y, angles roughly equal (~55°) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.4. BRONCHIAL ANATOMY

* Right main bronchus ~2.5cm long before branching [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Right UL bronchus departs at ~90° from right main bronchus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* In 10%, RUL bronchus <2.5cm from carina; 2-3% RUL bronchus opens into trachea above carina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * ⚠️ Special precaution needed when placing double-lumen ETT [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Left main bronchus ~5cm long before branching [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.5. BRONCHIOLES

* ~1mm diameter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* No cartilaginous support [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Highest proportion of smooth muscle in walls [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Terminal bronchiole is last airway component incapable of gas exchange [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Understanding airway anatomy is crucial for proper ETT placement, recognizing anatomical variants, and anticipating positions that may lead to endobronchial intubation or disconnection.

## 2.3. AIRWAY RESISTANCE
**Factors determining resistance to airflow through the respiratory tract.**

### 2.3.1. KEY PRINCIPLE

* Total cross-sectional area increases from central to peripheral airways → resistance drops [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Thus, airflow tends to be laminar in the peripheral airways and turbulent in central airways.

### 2.3.2. DEPENDENCY FACTORS

* Flow of gas per unit time [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Type of flow (laminar vs turbulent) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.3.3. LAMINAR FLOW

* Directly proportional to airway driving pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Assumes straight, unbranched airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Resistance inversely proportional to flow rate (Hagen-Poiseuille equation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Resistance = (8 × length × viscosity)/(π × radius⁴) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* $R = [8 × length × (viscosity)]/[\pi × (radius)^4]$ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.3.4. TURBULENT FLOW

* Occurs at high flow rates, branch points, angles, changing diameters [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Air flow proportional to square of airway driving pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Resistance increases proportionally to flow rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* More prominent frictional forces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.3.5. REYNOLDS NUMBER

* Determines flow type: laminar (<2,000), transitional (2,000-4,000), turbulent (>4,000) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reynolds number = (density × diameter × velocity)/viscosity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* $Reynold's number = V × d × gas density/gas viscosity$ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Helium has lower density than air, increases likelihood of laminar flow with narrowed airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Airway resistance depends on flow characteristics (laminar vs turbulent), with resistance proportional to the 4th power of radius reduction in laminar flow, explaining why small airway narrowing dramatically increases work of breathing.

## 2.4. TISSUE RESISTANCE
**Resistance from deformation of lung and chest wall tissues during breathing.**

* **Contribution**: up to half of total airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanism**: tissues (lung and chest wall) resist deformation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Variable factors**: proportion between lung/chest wall resistance changes based on scenario [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Pneumoperitoneum or increased intra-abdominal pressure increases chest wall resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Tissue resistance contributes significantly to total respiratory system resistance and is particularly affected by conditions that alter chest wall mechanics.

## 2.5. MEASUREMENT OF RESISTANCE
**Clinical approaches to quantify airway and tissue resistance.**

### 2.5.1. FORMULA

* Resistance = ΔP/Flow (cmH2O per L/sec) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* R_rs = \Delta P/Flow rate of inflation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 2.5.2. VENTILATOR MEASUREMENT

* Using inspiratory pause and pressure-time waveform [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Airway resistance: ΔP between peak pressure and first pressure drop (P1) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Tissue resistance: ΔP between P1 and P2 (more gradual decline) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Simplification: set inspiratory flow to 60L/min (1 L/sec) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Most accurate during paralysis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_2.png_57c27e.png_cb6815._.png)

### 2.5.3. RESISTANCE CHANGES DURING ANESTHESIA

**Airway Diameter Changes**
* Reduced diameter increases resistance (laryngospasm, glottic edema, kinked ETT) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Lung Volume Changes**
* Reduced volume decreases radial stress on bronchioles → narrowing → increased resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Normal inverse association between lung volume and resistance differs in COPD where hyperinflation compresses small airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Closing capacity increases linearly with age [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Gas Flow Changes**
* High expiratory flow reduces airway pressure (Bernoulli principle) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Resistance can be measured clinically using pressure-flow relationships during mechanical ventilation, with changes during anesthesia primarily from reduced lung volume and airway caliber alterations.

## 2.6. INTRINSIC PEEP
**Additional end-expiratory pressure from incomplete exhalation.**

### 2.6.1. DEFINITION

* Additional end-expiratory pressure beyond what is applied to patient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.6.2. CAUSES

* Incomplete exhalation due to flow limitation or airway closure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.6.3. DETERMINING FACTORS

* Expiratory time constant = product of resistance × compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Approximately 3 time constants required for lung emptying [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Interrupted exhalation → higher volume → increased alveolar pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.6.4. DETECTION

* Flow-time curve shows flow not returning to zero before next breath [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 2.6.5. PROGRESSION

* Decreased respiratory system compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Higher airway pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Eventually systemic hypotension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Intrinsic PEEP results from incomplete exhalation, creating additional end-expiratory pressure that can increase work of breathing, reduce venous return, and compromise hemodynamics if severe.

# 3. GAS EXCHANGE





## 3.1. FUNCTIONAL UNIT AND V/Q MATCHING
**The relationship between ventilation and perfusion that determines gas exchange efficiency.**

### 3.1.1. FUNCTIONAL UNIT

* Alveolus and surrounding capillaries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.1.2. ESSENTIAL SYSTEMS

* Ventilation (V): gas transport in alveolar space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Perfusion (Q): blood flow in capillaries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.1.3. V/Q RATIO

* Normal overall V/Q = 0.8 (V = 4L/min, CO = 5L/min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Heterogeneous throughout lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Healthy individuals: majority of alveoli between 0.8-1.0 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.1.4. V/Q MISMATCH CATEGORIES

* Alveoli with V>Q approaching ∞: dead space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Alveoli with Q>V approaching 0: shunt [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Alveoli with minimal Q and V: silent units [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Heterogeneity in the lung](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_3.png_dbd844.png_3a2054._.png)

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_4.png_2dce83.png_2b55f8._.png)

### 3.1.5. RILEY AND COURNAND MODEL

* Local V/Q ratios depend on gravity, lung structure, regional mechanics, lung injury [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dependent lung regions receive more V and Q than non-dependent regions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pleural pressure higher (transpulmonary pressure lower) in dependent areas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.1.6. JOHN WEST GRAVITATIONAL MODEL

* Zone 1 (uppermost): alveolar pressure > pulmonary arterial/venous pressure (low Q) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Zone 2 (middle): pulmonary arterial pressure > alveolar pressure > venous pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Zone 3 (lower): pulmonary arterial/venous pressure > alveolar pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Non-gravitational mechanisms (inherent architecture, prior blood flow) likely more important than gravity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_5.png_230d66.png_c62f9d._.png)


* Multiple inert gas elimination technique (MIGET) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Electrical impedance tomography (non-invasive bedside option) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Optimal gas exchange requires balanced ventilation and perfusion, with V/Q mismatching resulting in either dead space (wasted ventilation) or shunt (wasted perfusion), both reducing respiratory efficiency.

![Comparative Schematic: Shunt vs. Dead Space](shunt_vs_dead_space.png)

## 3.2. IMPAIRMENTS IN GAS EXCHANGE DURING ANESTHESIA
**How anesthesia affects oxygen and carbon dioxide exchange.**

### 3.2.1. GENERAL EFFECT

* Anesthesia induces more pronounced V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 3.2.2. OXYGEN CONSIDERATIONS

**Alveolar Gas Equation**
* PAO2 = FiO2(Patm - PH2O) - (PaCO2/0.8) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* P_A O_2 = F_i O_2 × (P_atm - P_H_2O) - P_a CO_2 × (VO_2/VCO_2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Components:
   * FiO2: inspired oxygen concentration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Patm: barometric pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * PH2O: water vapor pressure (usually 47 cmH2O) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * PaCO2: CO2 concentration in blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * 0.8: respiratory quotient (CO2 production/O2 consumption) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**A-a Gradient**
* Normal difference between PAO2 and PaO2 is 5-10 mmHg (physiologic shunt) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Widens with gas exchange impairments [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**P/F Ratio**
* PaO2:FiO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Defines ARDS but has limitations [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Varying FiO2 changes P/F ratio depending on shunt amount [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 3.2.3. MECHANISMS OF HYPOXEMIA IN ANESTHESIA

**V/Q Mismatch (Usually Shunt)**
* Venous admixture or shunt fraction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Shunt equation: QS/QT = (CCO2 - CaO2)/(CCO2 - CVO2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* $Q_s/Q_t = (CCO_2 - CaO_2)/(CCO_2 - CVO_2)$ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Components:
   * CaO2: arterial blood oxygen content [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * CVO2: mixed venous blood oxygen content [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * CCO2: end-capillary blood oxygen content [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: With increased shunting, arterial oxygenation more impacted by PVO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Treatment: reverse shunt if possible; if not, increase mixed venous oxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Hypoxic Pulmonary Vasoconstriction (HPV)**
* Response to alveolar hypoxia and low PVO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pulmonary arteries vasoconstrict to maintain V/Q matching [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Seen in one-lung ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 🔑 **HY**: HPV inhibited by inhalational anesthetics and high FiO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Diffusion Limitation** 
- In exercise, time in capillary decreases which decreases time for diffusion (although in normal circumstances it is inconsequential)

**Bottom Line:** Anesthesia disrupts V/Q matching primarily through atelectasis and shunt, with hypoxemia exacerbated by inhibition of hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction by inhalational agents.

## 3.3. CO2 EXCHANGE
**Principles governing carbon dioxide transport and elimination.**

### 3.3.1. ALVEOLAR CO2 DETERMINATION

* PACO2 = (VCO2 × 0.863)/VA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* $P_A CO_2 = VCO_2/V_A × K where K is a constant.$ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Components:
   * VCO2: CO2 production [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * VA: alveolar ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * 0.863: conversion factor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.2. ALVEOLAR VENTILATION

* Closely linked to alveolar ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Not the same as minute ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* MV = RR × Vt (encompasses alveolar ventilation and deadspace ventilation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.3. DEAD SPACE COMPONENTS

* **Anatomic dead space**: nose, pharynx, conducting airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Measured by Fowler's Method
* **Alveolar dead space**: alveoli with minimal blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Physiological dead space** = Anatomic dead space + Alveolar dead space
* **Instrumental dead space**: masks, ETT, connectors, HME [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Often overlooked but easily modified [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.4. DEAD SPACE QUANTIFICATION

* Enghoff modification of Bohr equation: Vd/Vt = (PaCO2 - PECO2)/PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* $V_d/V_t = (P_a CO_2 - P_E CO_2)/P_a CO_2$ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PaCO2: assumed similar to PACO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PECO2: partial pressure of CO2 in mixed expired gas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal Vd/Vt ≈ 0.33 in spontaneously breathing individuals [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Up to 0.5 in healthy individuals on PPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** CO2 elimination depends primarily on alveolar ventilation, with dead space (anatomic, alveolar, and instrumental) representing the portion of ventilation that doesn't participate in gas exchange.

## 3.4. CONTROL OF BREATHING
**Neural mechanisms regulating respiratory drive and pattern.**

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_6.png_f065c5.png_58ecad._.png)

### 3.4.1. CENTRAL CONTROL

* Main rhythm generator in PreBotzinger Complex (ventrolateral medulla) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Signals travel to spinal motor neurons (C-spine) to diaphragm NMJ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.4.2. MAJOR INPUTS

* Cortical [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Chemical [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reflexive feedback [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.4.3. CHEMICAL FEEDBACK

**CO2 Response**
* Main stimulant is PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Central chemoreceptors on ventrolateral medulla surface [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* CO2 crosses BBB → increases H+ ions in CSF → decreases pH → increases RR → increases MV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * MV increases by 2-3L/min per mmHg of PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PaCO2 at steady state determined by intersection of PaCO2-ventilation response curve and metabolic hyperbola [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Below apneic threshold, ventilation reduced/may cease in sleeping/sedated individuals [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Ventilatory response to elevated CO2 declines as CSF pH normalizes (seen in chronic CO2 retention) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_7.png_699a7a.png_4a8948._.png)

**O2 Response**
* Weaker modulator [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Sensed by peripheral chemoreceptors at carotid bifurcation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 3.4.4. REFLEXIVE CONTROL

* Primarily prevents airway obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Vomiting Reflex**
* Coordinates swallowing, salivation, GI reflexes, ventilatory movements, diaphragmatic/abdominal muscle activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Inspiration inhibited during vomiting to prevent aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Input from cranial and spinal nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Cough Reflex**
* Stimulated by tracheal subepithelium (especially posterior wall and carina) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Deep inspiration → forced exhalation against closed glottis → increased intrathoracic pressure → expulsive maneuver [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Proprioception**
* Smooth muscle stretch receptors in airways (sense pressure not volume) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Also in intercostal muscles (Golgi tendon organs) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Hering-Breuer Reflex**
* Decreases ventilatory effort during sustained lung distention [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Blocked by bilateral vagotomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Only weakly present in healthy adults [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Breathing control involves central pattern generators modified by chemical feedback (primarily CO2) and protective reflexes, with numerous influences including cortical input and proprioceptive feedback.

## 3.5. CO2 EXCHANGE UNDER ANESTHESIA
**Effects of anesthesia on carbon dioxide handling.**

### 3.5.1. IMPAIRMENT MECHANISMS

**Changes in Alveolar Ventilation**
* Drugs disrupt ventilatory control (dose-dependent) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: During apnea, PaCO2 increases ~10 mmHg first minute, 3.5 mmHg/min after [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* High concentrations of inhaled anesthetics/hypnotics depress response to hypoxemia/hypercarbia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Opiates cause right-shift of PaCO2-ventilation response curve and higher resting PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**V/Q Mismatch**
* Increased dead space under GA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Instrumental dead space from breathing devices/connectors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Increased alveolar dead space from ventilation inhomogeneity and alveolar overdistension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Widens alveolar-arterial CO2 gradient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Effect of shunt on PaCO2 less significant than on PaO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**CO2 Production**
* Generally decreases during GA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Can increase in hypermetabolic states (shivering, fever, malignant hyperthermia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* May increase with sodium bicarbonate administration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Anesthesia alters CO2 handling primarily through ventilatory control depression, increased dead space from V/Q mismatch, and variable changes in CO2 production depending on metabolic state.

# 4. LUNG VOLUMES AND CAPACITIES





## 4.1. DEFINITIONS AND NORMAL VALUES
**Standard measurements of lung volumes and capacities.**

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_8.png_1d3335.png_0c5952._.png)

### 4.1.1. TIDAL VOLUME (VT)

* 6-8ml/kg during quiet breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Falls with decreased lung compliance or ventilatory muscle strength [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.2. VITAL CAPACITY (VC)

* ~60ml/kg (may vary ±20% in healthy individuals) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Decreased by restrictive pulmonary diseases or extrapulmonary conditions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.3. INSPIRATORY CAPACITY (IC)

* Largest volume inspired from resting expiratory level [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Decreases with significant extrathoracic airway obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Usually changes parallel to VC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.4. EXPIRATORY RESERVE VOLUME (ERV)

* Volume within lungs after forced maximal expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Limited diagnostic value [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.5. LUNG VOLUMES VS. LUNG CAPACITIES

* **Lung volume**: Measured directly by spirometer or gas dilution technique [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Lung capacity**: Sum of two or more lung volumes; a derived value [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Four volumes**: Tidal volume, inspiratory reserve volume, expiratory reserve volume, residual volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Four capacities**: Functional residual capacity, vital capacity, inspiratory capacity, total lung capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal values (70-kg man, standing position):
  * Tidal volume (VT) = 500 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inspiratory reserve volume (IRV) = 2500 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Expiratory reserve volume (ERV) = 1500 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Residual volume = 1500 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Functional residual capacity (FRC) = 3000 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Vital capacity (VC) = 4500 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inspiratory capacity (IC) = 3000 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Total lung capacity (TLC) = 6000 mL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Standardized lung volumes describe different aspects of pulmonary function, with values affected by intrinsic lung properties, respiratory muscle strength, and disease states.

## 4.2. PULMONARY FUNCTION TESTS
**Clinical assessment of lung function through standardized measurements.**

### 4.2.1. FORCED VITAL CAPACITY (FVC)

* Largest volume expired after maximal inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Global measure of respiratory capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reduced in chronic obstructive diseases and restrictive diseases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 🔑 **HY**: FVC <15ml/kg associated with PPCs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Single breath count correlates with FVC (simple bedside test) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 4.2.2. FORCED EXPIRATORY VOLUME (FEV)

* FEVT is forced expiratory volume over time interval during FVC maneuver [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Measure of flow, decreased in obstructive and restrictive patterns [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Most important application: comparison with FVC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal values:
   * FEV0.5: >50-60% of FVC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * FEV1: >75-85% of FVC (or FEV1/FVC >0.75) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * FEV2: >94% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * FEV3: >97% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 🔑 **HY**: Restrictive diseases have normal FEV1/FVC ratios; obstructive diseases have reduced FEV1/FVC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 4.2.3. FORCED EXPIRATORY FLOW (FEF)

* FEF25%-75%: average flow during middle half of FEV maneuver [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal for 70kg man: ~4.7 L/sec (280 L/min) or 100±25% predicted [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Decreased flow rates indicate medium airways obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Normal in restrictive diseases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* More reliable than FEV1/FVC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_9.png_78f131.png_7eabcc._.png)

### 4.2.4. FLOW-VOLUME LOOPS

* Demonstrates flow over volume during respiratory cycle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Loop configuration most informative part [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In mechanical ventilation, loop usually inverted [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Previously used to diagnose large/extrathoracic airway obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_10.png_124432.png_efc2b0_.png)

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_image_11.png_ea9d09.png_e67c28_.png)

### 4.2.5. DLCO (CO DIFFUSING CAPACITY)

* Uses gas mixture containing CO to measure diffusing capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PPCO nearly 0, affinity for Hb 200× greater than O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal: ~25ml CO/min/mmHg (single breath method at rest) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Can increase 2-3× during exercise [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* DLO2 estimated by DLCO ×1.23 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Influenced by:
   * Hb concentration (decreased Hb decreases DLCO) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Alveolar PCO2 (increased PaCO2 increases DLCO) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Supine position (increases DLCO) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
   * Pulmonary capillary blood volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Decreased in diffusion defects (alveolar fibrosis, sarcoidosis, asbestosis, pulmonary edema) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Low DLCO more related to loss of lung volume or capillary bed perfusion than true diffusion impairment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

| Value | Restrictive Disease | Obstructive Disease |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Definition | Proportional decreases in all lung volumes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | Small airway obstruction to expiratory flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| FVC | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | Normal or slightly ↑ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| FEV₁ | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | Normal or slightly ↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| FEV₁/FVC | Normal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| FEF₂₅₋₇₅% | Normal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| FRC | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | Normal or ↑ if gas trapping [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |
| TLC | ↓↓↓ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] | Normal or ↑ if gas trapping [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] |

FEV, forced expiratory volume; FRC, functional residual capacity; FVC, forced vital capacity; TLC, total lung capacity; ↓↓↓, ↑↑↑ = large decrease or increase, respectively; ↓, ↑ = small/moderate decrease or increase, respectively.

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary function tests differentiate between obstructive and restrictive patterns, with obstructive diseases showing reduced flow rates but preserved volumes, while restrictive diseases show proportionally reduced volumes with preserved flow rates.

## 4.3. FUNCTIONAL RESIDUAL CAPACITY (FRC)

### 4.3.1. IMPORTANCE OF FRC

* Starting point of tidal breathing - balance point between elastic forces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Serves as oxygen buffer during normal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Prevents alveolar collapse during normal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Maintains optimal lung compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Critical oxygen reservoir during apnea (e.g., during anesthetic induction) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* FRC below closing capacity leads to small airway closure and V̇/Q̇ mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.3.2. FACTORS AFFECTING FRC

* **FRC Reduction Factors**:
  * Supine position (falls by ~1000 mL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased intra-abdominal pressure (obesity, pregnancy, pneumoperitoneum) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * General anesthesia (loss of thoracic cage muscle tone) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Young age (neonates, infants, children) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lung disease (fibrosis, pulmonary edema, atelectasis, ARDS) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **FRC Increase Factors**:
  * PEEP application [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Emphysema (reduced elastic recoil)
  * Increasing age (reduced elastic tissue) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Asthma (air trapping, high intrinsic PEEP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.3.3. MEASUREMENT METHODS

* Cannot be directly measured by spirometry (includes residual volume) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Three calculation methods:
  * Gas dilution [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Body plethysmography [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Multiple-breath N2 washout [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.3.4. GAS DILUTION METHOD

* Patient breathes helium through a spirometer at end of tidal expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Helium concentration drops as it equilibrates with all lung gas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* FRC calculated from: FRC = V1(C1-C2)/C2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* May underestimate lung volumes when airways are closed (e.g., COPD) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.3.5. BODY PLETHYSMOGRAPHY

* Measures all gas within lungs, including trapped gas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Patient sits in airtight box with pressure transducers [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Based on Boyle's law (PV = constant)
* Mouthpiece shutter closes at end expiration; patient attempts inhalation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Respiratory effort increases thoracic volume, decreasing box volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* FRC calculated from pressure changes: FRC = P4ΔV/(P3-P4) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 4.3.6. MULTIPLE-BREATH NITROGEN WASHOUT

* Patient breathes 100% O2 (instead of room air) at end of tidal expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Expired gases collected and analyzed for N2 concentration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Test continues until expired N2 concentration <1% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* FRC calculation: FRC = total expired N2 volume × [N2]f/[N2]i [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Also underestimates volume with gas trapping [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** FRC is a critical physiological parameter representing the balance point between opposing elastic forces of the lung and chest wall. It serves multiple functions including oxygen reservoir and prevention of alveolar collapse, with numerous factors affecting its volume.

## 4.4. CLOSING CAPACITY

### 4.4.1. DEFINITION AND SIGNIFICANCE

* Volume at which small airways begin to close during expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Occurs when pressure outside airways exceeds internal pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Closing capacity = residual volume + closing volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal relationship with FRC:
  * Young adults: CC below FRC (no airway closure during tidal breathing) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * With age: CC increases, exceeding FRC at ~65-70 years standing, ~40 years supine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* When CC exceeds FRC, airway closure during tidal breathing causes V̇/Q̇ mismatch and hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increases in 
	* COPD (due to small airway closure, less lung elasticity and radial traction during expiration), 
	* Increasing age and may exceed FRC in supine position above 45yo and standing position above 65yo
	* Smoking
	* Neonates (cartilaginous ribcage, less outward recoil of chest wall which normally contributes to radial traction during expiration)
* Decreases under anaesthesia (FRC and CC decreases by same extent)
* No change in obesity, pregnancy and supine position

### 4.4.2. MEASUREMENT OF CLOSING CAPACITY

![Closing Capacity vs FRC](closing_capacity_vs_frc.png)

* Measured using Fowler's method (single-breath N2 washout) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Closing capacity is the lung volume at which small airways begin to close, and when it exceeds FRC (as occurs with aging, supine position, or disease), it results in V̇/Q̇ mismatch during normal breathing, predisposing to hypoxemia.

# 5. POSTOPERATIVE PULMONARY COMPLICATIONS (PPC) RISK FACTORS





## 5.1. DEMOGRAPHIC FACTORS AND PRE-EXISTING CONDITIONS
**Patient characteristics that increase risk of postoperative pulmonary complications.**

| **Risk Factors**             | **Details**                                                                                                           |     |
| ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
| Morbid obesity               | BMI >40 kg/m² [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                                | ⓘ]] |
| Current smoking              | Within 8 weeks of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                    | ⓘ]] |
| Chronic lung disease         | COPD, asthma, interstitial lung disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation      | ⓘ]] |
| Older age                    | >65 years; risk increases with advancing age [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation | ⓘ]] |
| Dependent functional status  | Inability to perform ADLs independently [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation      | ⓘ]] |
| Recent respiratory infection | ~4 weeks prior to surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                    | ⓘ]] |
| Malignancy                   | Particularly with cachexia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                   | ⓘ]] |
| Male sex                     | Higher risk than female [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                      | ⓘ]] |
| Anemia                       | <10 g/dL [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation                                     | ⓘ]] |

## 5.2. PROCEDURE FACTORS
**Surgical and anesthetic factors that increase risk of postoperative pulmonary complications.**

| **Risk Factors** | **Details** |
| --- | --- |
| Intrathoracic surgery | Directly affects respiratory mechanics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]] |
| Longer duration of surgery | >3 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]] |
| Emergency procedure | Limited time for optimization [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]] |

🔑 **HY**: ASA status is the strongest predictor of PPCs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Postoperative pulmonary complications are influenced by patient factors (obesity, smoking, chronic lung disease, age) and surgical factors (thoracic procedures, lengthy operations), with ASA status being the strongest overall predictor.

# 6. THE UPPER AND LOWER AIRWAYS




## 6.1. UPPER RESPIRATORY TRACT COMPONENTS AND FUNCTIONS
**The components and functions of the upper respiratory tract in ventilation and other processes.**

## 6.2. AIRWAY PATENCY DURING BREATHING
**Mechanisms that maintain airway patency during normal breathing.**

### 6.2.1. MECHANISMS OF AIRWAY PATENCY

* Diaphragm contraction creates negative airway pressure during inspiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Large airways protected from collapse by cartilaginous support [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Pharynx lacks cartilage support, relying instead on muscle activity to prevent collapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 6.2.2. MUSCLES MAINTAINING PHARYNGEAL PATENCY

* **Genioglossus**: Main pharyngeal dilator, pushes tongue forward/away from pharyngeal wall [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Palatal muscles**: Control stiffness/position of palate, tongue, pharynx, and uvula shape [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Hyoid position muscles**: Geniohyoid shows phasic activity, increasing during inspiration to stiffen/dilate upper airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Conscious state maintains patent airway even with pressures as low as -60 cmH2O [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Airway patency is maintained by a balance between negative inspiratory pressure and dilator muscle activity, with the pharynx being the most vulnerable area for collapse due to lack of cartilaginous support.

## 6.3. AIRWAY DURING SLEEP
**Changes in upper airway function during sleep and sleep-related breathing disorders.**

### 6.3.1. SLEEP-RELATED CHANGES

* Pharyngeal dilator muscle tone significantly decreases during sleep [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Greatest loss of muscle tone occurs during stage 3 NREM (non-rapid eye movement) sleep
* Most individuals maintain airway patency despite reduced muscle tone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Susceptible individuals may experience:
   * Partial obstruction causing snoring (affects ~30% of population) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Complete obstruction leading to obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 6.3.2. OBSTRUCTIVE SLEEP APNEA

* **Definition**: Recurrent episodes of complete upper airway obstruction during deep sleep [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Pathophysiology**:
   * Pharyngeal collapse despite continued diaphragmatic effort [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Apneic periods typically last 20-40 seconds [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Results in hypoxia and hypercapnia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Chemoreceptor stimulation causes arousal from sleep [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Cycle repeats with return to deep sleep [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Consequences**:
   * Neuropsychiatric: daytime sleepiness, poor concentration, irritability, anxiety, depression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Endocrinological: impaired glucose tolerance, dyslipidemia, increased ACTH and cortisol [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Cardiovascular: hypertension, atrial fibrillation, myocardial infarction, stroke [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Risk Factors**:
   * Anatomical: craniofacial abnormalities, tonsillar/adenoidal hypertrophy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Obesity: fat deposition around pharynx; decreased FRC exacerbates hypoxemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Male gender: increased pharyngeal fat deposition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Treatment**:
   * Lifestyle modification: weight loss, smoking cessation, alcohol reduction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Mouth devices [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * nCPAP (nasal Continuous Positive Airway Pressure): +5 to +20 cmH2O pneumatic splint [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Benefits: reduces daytime sleepiness and atrial fibrillation; improves mood, cognitive function, and blood pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 6.3.3. PERIOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT OF OSA

* **Screening**: Use STOP-BANG questionnaire (Snoring, Tiredness, Observed apneas, Pressure/hypertension, BMI>35, Age>50, Neck size>40cm, Gender=male) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Anesthetic Considerations**:
   * Avoid sedative premedication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Use regional/local techniques when possible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Preoxygenate in sitting position to maximize FRC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Consider using CPAP or HFNO for preoxygenation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Prefer endotracheal intubation over LMA for airway security [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Anticipate difficult laryngoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * For extubation: reverse neuromuscular blockade fully, position for maximum FRC, ensure full wakefulness
   * Use post-operative CPAP and multi-modal analgesia to minimize opioid use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Sleep significantly reduces pharyngeal muscle tone, potentially causing airway obstruction in susceptible individuals. OSA is characterized by recurrent complete airway collapse during sleep, requiring special perioperative management to reduce complications from anesthetic and sedative drugs.

## 6.4. HIGH-FLOW NASAL OXYGEN THERAPY
**Principles and applications of high-flow nasal oxygen delivery systems.**

### 6.4.1. CONVENTIONAL OXYGEN LIMITATIONS

* Cold, dry oxygen limited to 15 L/min maximum flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Masks often fit poorly, causing entrainment of air and skin damage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Patient peak inspiratory flow may exceed oxygen flow, causing further entrainment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Dry oxygen delivery leads to thick secretions and impaired mucociliary function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Low-flow nasal cannulae improve compliance but flows >4 L/min are poorly tolerated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 6.4.2. HFNO BENEFITS

* Delivers warmed, humidified oxygen at flows up to 60 L/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Provides inspired oxygen concentrations up to 100% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Generates positive end-expiratory pressure of +3 to +7 cmH2O (mouth-dependent) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Provides pharyngeal dead space washout, reducing effective anatomical dead space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Reduces metabolic cost of breathing by eliminating need to warm and humidify gases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 6.4.3. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

* Hypoxemic respiratory failure in critical care settings [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Perioperative applications:
   * Preoxygenation: provides denitrogenation and oxygen reservoir during laryngoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Particularly useful for patients with reduced FRC (obese, obstetric, acute abdomen) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Intraoperative oxygenation during sedation or tubeless airway surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
   * Post-extubation to re-recruit collapsed alveoli and prevent upper airway collapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** HFNO therapy overcomes limitations of conventional oxygen delivery systems by providing high flows of warmed, humidified oxygen, generating PEEP, washing out dead space, and reducing work of breathing, with expanding applications in perioperative care.

**Standardized measurements of lung function and their interpretation.**

**Principles of oxygen carriage in the blood and delivery to tissues.**

## 6.11. RBC STRUCTURE AND HEMOGLOBIN
**Structure and function of red blood cells and hemoglobin in oxygen transport.**

## 6.12. ABNORMAL HEMOGLOBIN FORMS
**Pathological forms of hemoglobin and their clinical implications.**

## 6.13. THE SHUNT EQUATION
**Mathematical description of pulmonary shunting and its effects on oxygenation.**

## 6.14. HALDANE EFFECT AND BOHR EFFECT

![Bohr vs Haldane Effect](haldane_vs_bohr.png)
**The relationship between oxygen and carbon dioxide transport in the blood.**

## 6.15. TYPES OF HYPOXIA
**Different mechanisms of tissue oxygen deficiency and their clinical manifestations.**

## 6.16. FACTORS AFFECTING DIFFUSION
**Physical principles governing gas diffusion across biological membranes.**

## 6.17. ALVEOLAR DESIGN FOR GAS DIFFUSION
**Anatomical features optimizing gas exchange in the lungs.**

### 6.17.1. ANATOMICAL ADAPTATIONS

* Large surface area: ~300 million alveoli providing 70 m² for gas exchange [A:40-41]
* Thin alveolar-capillary barrier: As little as 200 nm in some places [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.17.2. DIFFUSION EFFICIENCY

* Average RBC transit time through pulmonary capillary: 0.75 seconds [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* O2 diffusion complete within 0.25 seconds at rest (threefold safety factor) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* High safety margin means hypoxemia rarely due to diffusion defect compared to V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The lungs are anatomically optimized for efficient gas exchange through an enormous surface area and minimal diffusion distance, providing substantial reserve for oxygen diffusion under normal conditions.

## 6.18. COMPARISON OF O2 AND CO2 DIFFUSION
**Differences in diffusion characteristics between oxygen and carbon dioxide.**

### 6.18.1. MOLECULAR WEIGHT COMPARISON

* O2: 32 Da [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* CO2: 44 Da [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Similar MWs would predict similar diffusion rates based on Graham's law alone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.18.2. SOLUBILITY EFFECTS

* CO2 solubility coefficient 20 times higher than O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in CO2 diffusion rate 20 times higher than O2 despite similar MW [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.18.3. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

* O2 diffusion more likely limited in pathological conditions (e.g., pulmonary fibrosis) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in Type 1 respiratory failure (hypoxemia without hypercapnia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Clinically significant hypercapnia never caused by impaired diffusion alone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Despite similar molecular weights, CO2 diffuses much more rapidly than O2 due to its higher solubility, explaining why diffusion defects typically cause hypoxemia without hypercapnia.

## 6.19. PERFUSION VS. DIFFUSION LIMITATION
**Factors determining whether gas exchange is limited by blood flow or membrane diffusion.**

### 6.19.1. OXYGEN DIFFUSION PROFILE

![Perfusion vs Diffusion Limitation](perfusion_vs_diffusion_limitation.png)

* O2 diffuses into plasma, increasing PO2 and reducing pressure gradient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Equilibrium reached between alveolar and plasma PO2 after 0.25s [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Under normal conditions, O2 transfer is perfusion limited [A:41-42]

### 6.19.2. CARBON MONOXIDE DIFFUSION PROFILE

* Binds to Hb with affinity 250× greater than O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Virtually no CO dissolved in plasma - plasma PCO remains very low [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Substantial pressure gradient persists throughout capillary transit [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Equilibrium never reached between alveolar and plasma PCO [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
