* CO transfer is diffusion limited [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Used for testing diffusing capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.19.3. OTHER GASES

* N2O and volatile anesthetics:
  * Do not bind to Hb [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Relatively insoluble [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Equilibrium rapidly reached (N2O within 0.075s) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Perfusion limited [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.19.4. CONDITIONS CAUSING O2 DIFFUSION LIMITATION

![Diffusion Limitation - Thickened Barrier](diffusion_limitation_thickened_barrier.png)

* **Thickened alveolar-capillary barrier** (e.g., pulmonary fibrosis):
  * Decreases diffusion rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Equilibrium not achieved by end of pulmonary capillary transit [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Exercise**:
  * Increased cardiac output reduces RBC transit time (extreme exercise: as little as 0.25s) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Normal alveolar-capillary barrier: just reaches equilibrium during available transit time [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Diseased barrier: exercise-induced transit time reduction causes hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Altitude**:
  * Lower barometric pressure reduces alveolar PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * O2 transfer becomes diffusion limited at lower threshold [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Patients with mild lung disease may develop impaired O2 diffusion at altitude [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Even normal lungs: intense exercise at altitude may cause diffusion limitation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Diffusion Limitation - Altitude](diffusion_limitation_altitude.png)

**Bottom Line:** Under normal conditions, oxygen transfer is perfusion-limited, but becomes diffusion-limited with alveolar-capillary barrier thickening, increased cardiac output/reduced transit time during exercise, or at altitude where pressure gradients are reduced.

## 6.20. LUNG DIFFUSION CAPACITY
**Measurement of the lung's ability to transfer gases across the alveolar-capillary membrane.**

### 6.20.1. DEFINITION

* Diffusion capacity of lung for carbon monoxide (DLCO or transfer factor/TLCO) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Measurement of lungs' ability to transfer gases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.20.2. METHODOLOGY

* Uses diffusion-limited gas (carbon monoxide) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Single vital capacity breath of 0.3% CO held for 10 seconds then exhaled [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Difference between inspired and expired PCO represents amount diffused [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Corrected for Hb concentration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Also affected by altitude, age, and sex [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.20.3. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

* Diagnoses disease of alveolar-capillary barrier [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reduced by:
  * Thickened barrier: pulmonary fibrosis, interstitial lung diseases, pulmonary edema [A:42-43]
  * Reduced surface area: emphysema, pulmonary embolus, pneumonectomy/lobectomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* For post-pneumonectomy/lobectomy patients: transfer coefficient (KCO) corrects for reduced alveolar volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased by:
  * Exercise (capillary recruitment and distension) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary hemorrhage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Asthma (may also be normal) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Obesity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 6.20.4. MANAGEMENT OF DIFFUSION IMPAIRMENT

* Main consequence: hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Management focuses on preventing/treating hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Anesthetist can influence:
  * Pressure gradient: Increase FiO2 to overcome thickened barrier [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * For acute pulmonary edema:
	* Apply PEEP to recruit collapsed alveoli (increases surface area) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* PEEP redistributes alveolar edema (reduces thickness) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Lung diffusion capacity (DLCO) measures gas transfer across the alveolar-capillary membrane using carbon monoxide, with reduced values indicating thickened barriers or decreased surface area, guiding clinical interventions to improve oxygenation.

# 7. VENTILATION AND DEAD SPACE




## 7.1. TYPES OF DEAD SPACE
**Different components of ventilated but unperfused respiratory space.**

## 7.2. FACTORS AFFECTING DEAD SPACE
**Physiological and pathological influences on ventilatory dead space.**

### 7.2.1. ANATOMICAL DEAD SPACE FACTORS

* **Normal value**: ~150mL in 70kg man (~2mL/kg or one-third of tidal volume) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Modifying factors**:
  * Patient size: Increases with lung size [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lung volume: Higher lung volumes increase airway diameter through radial traction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Posture: Supine position decreases lung volume, reducing airway diameter and VAnatD [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Bronchoconstriction: Reduces airway diameter, decreases VAnatD [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Bronchodilation: Increases airway diameter, increases VAnatD [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 7.2.2. ALVEOLAR DEAD SPACE FACTORS

* **Normal value**: Negligible in healthy lungs with well-matched ventilation and perfusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Increasing factors**:
  * Upright posture: Gravity affects West zone 2 in lung apices, creating ventilated but inadequately perfused alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Low pulmonary artery pressure: Reduced right ventricular output causes insufficient apical perfusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Positive pressure ventilation/PEEP: Increases alveolar pressure, compressing apical vessels (West zone 1) and reducing venous return [A:45-46]
  * Pulmonary embolism: Creates ventilated but unperfused alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * COPD: Destruction of alveolar septa reduces gas exchange surface area [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 7.2.3. MECHANICAL DEAD SPACE

* **Definition**: Part of anesthetic breathing system containing exhaled gases at end-expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Examples**: Circuit components, facemasks, ETT, connectors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical significance**:
  * Inevitable in all airway devices [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Risk of rebreathing if mechanical dead space exceeds tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Requires increased minute ventilation to maintain alveolar ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Particularly important in pediatric anesthesia where dead space may be proportionally larger [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Should be minimized by cutting ETTs, reducing catheter mount length, minimizing angle pieces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Dead space varies with patient factors (size, position), physiologic conditions (lung volume, bronchomotor tone), pathological states (PE, COPD), and equipment choices (circuit components), with minimization of mechanical dead space being particularly important in pediatric anesthesia.

## 7.3. DEAD SPACE VENTILATION
**Dead space as a component of total ventilation.**

### 7.3.1. VENTILATION COMPONENTS

* **Minute ventilation (V̇E)**: Volume of air inspired per minute [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇E = VT × RR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * RR: Respiratory rate (breaths per minute) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Alveolar ventilation (V̇A)**: Portion of V̇E participating in gas exchange [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇A = VA × RR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇A = (VT - VD) × RR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Dead space ventilation (V̇D)**: Portion of V̇E not participating in gas exchange [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇D = VD × RR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇D = (VT - VA) × RR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Overall: V̇E = V̇A + V̇D [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Minute ventilation divides into alveolar ventilation (participates in gas exchange) and dead space ventilation (wasted ventilation), with the relationship V̇E = V̇A + V̇D.

## 7.4. ALVEOLAR VENTILATION AND CO2
**Relationship between alveolar ventilation and arterial carbon dioxide tension.**

### 7.4.1. INVERSE RELATIONSHIP

* Inverse mathematical relationship between PaCO2 and V̇A [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![image.png](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_MV_on_PaCO2_exercise.001.jpeg_.jpeg)

### 7.4.2. MATHEMATICAL FORMULA

* PaCO2 = K × V̇CO2/V̇A [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* V̇CO2: Rate of CO2 production through metabolism [A:46-47]
* K: Correction factor for dissimilar units of V̇CO2 (mL/min), V̇A (L/min), and PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* If PaCO2 measured in kilopascals, K = 0.015 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 7.4.3. CLINICAL PRINCIPLES

* Doubling V̇A halves PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased metabolic rate without compensatory V̇A increase causes hypercapnia (e.g., malignant hyperthermia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Alveolar ventilation (not minute ventilation) determines PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 7.4.4. HYPERVENTILATION EXAMPLE

* Comparing two patients with different breathing patterns but identical V̇E (7500mL):
  * Patient 1: VT 500mL, RR 15/min, VAnatD 150mL → V̇A = (500-150)×15 = 5250mL
  * Patient 2: VT 250mL, RR 30/min, VAnatD 150mL → V̇A = (250-150)×30 = 3000mL
  * Patient 1 will have lower PaCO2 due to higher V̇A despite identical V̇E [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Arterial CO2 tension is inversely proportional to alveolar ventilation, with important clinical implications for patients with different breathing patterns despite similar minute ventilation.

## 7.5. MEASUREMENT METHODS
**Techniques for measuring anatomical and physiological dead space.**

### 7.5.1. FOWLER'S METHOD (ANATOMICAL DEAD SPACE)

* **Technique**: Single-breath nitrogen washout [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Procedure**:
  * Patient breathes tidal volumes of room air initially [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Takes vital capacity breath of 100% O2 at end of normal tidal expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Expires slowly to maximal expiration into spirometer with N2 analyzer [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Results**: Plot of expired N2 against expired gas volume showing four phases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Phase 1: Anatomical dead space gas (pure O2, no N2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Phase 2: Mixed dead-space and alveolar gas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Phase 3: Alveolar plateau [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Phase 4: Sharp N2 increase at closing capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Calculation**: Midpoint of phase 2 curve (where area A equals area B) represents estimation of Anatomical Dead Space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Fowlers Method.001.jpeg](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_Fowlers_Method.001.jpeg_c985c_.jpeg)

### 7.5.2. BOHR METHOD (PHYSIOLOGICAL DEAD SPACE)

* **Formula**: VD/VT = (PaCO2 - PETCO2)/PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Components**:
  * PaCO2: Arterial tension of CO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PETCO2: End-tidal tension of CO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Procedure**: Requires arterial blood gas and end-tidal CO2 measurement at same time [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Normal value**: VD/VT = 0.2-0.35 during tidal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Bohr Equation](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_Bohr_Equation.001.jpeg_096ef3_.jpeg)

### 7.5.3. DERIVATION OF BOHR EQUATION

* Based on principle that all CO2 comes from ventilated and perfused alveoli, none from dead space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Starting with VT = VA + VD [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Define FA as alveolar CO2 fraction, FE as expired CO2 fraction, FD as dead space CO2 fraction [A:48-49]
* VT × FE = VA × FA + VD × FD [A:48-49]
* Since dead space contains no CO2, FD = 0 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* VT × FE = VA × FA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Substituting VA = VT - VD [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* VT × FE = (VT - VD) × FA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* VT × FE = VT × FA - VD × FA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* VD × FA = VT(FA - FE) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* VD/VT = (FA - FE)/FA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Using partial pressures (proportional to concentrations): VD/VT = (PACO2 - PECO2)/PACO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* For practical measurement: PACO2 ≈ PaCO2 and PECO2 ≈ PETCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Final simplified Bohr equation: VD/VT = (PaCO2 - PETCO2)/PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Anatomical dead space is measured using Fowler's single-breath nitrogen washout method, while physiological dead space is calculated using the Bohr equation, requiring simultaneous measurement of arterial and end-tidal CO2.

# 8. RESPIRATORY ANATOMY




## 8.1. THE MOUTH AND PALATE
**Key structures in the oral cavity relevant to airway management and intubation.**

## 8.2. THE NOSE
**Anatomical structures of the nasal cavity relevant to anesthesia practice.**

### 8.2.1. EXTERNAL NOSE AND NASAL CAVITY

* **External nose**: Formed by nasal bones, frontal processes of maxillae, and cartilages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Nasal cavity**: Divided by septum into separate compartments, each with:
  * Roof: Sloping upward (bridge), horizontal (cribriform plate), downward (sphenoid) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Floor: Palatine process of maxilla and horizontal plate of palatine bone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Medial wall: Nasal septum (septal cartilage, perpendicular plate of ethmoid, vomer) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Deviated septum present in ~75% of adults, usually from minor trauma in childhood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lateral wall: Contains three conchae (turbinates) - superior, middle, inferior [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Each concha arches over a meatus (air passage) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.2. PARANASAL SINUSES

* Air-filled extensions of nasal cavity developing during childhood and adolescence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Maxillary sinus** (antrum of Highmore):
  * Largest sinus, pyramid-shaped, occupies body of maxilla [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Floor extends into alveolar process ~1.25cm below floor of nose [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Roots of molars may create bulges in floor or perforate it [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Drains into middle meatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Sphenoid sinuses**:
  * Within body of sphenoid, unequal in size, may extend into basisphenoid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Open into spheno-ethmoidal recess [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Frontal sinuses**:
  * Above orbits/root of nose in frontal bone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Usually unequal in size [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Drain through frontonasal duct into middle meatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Ethmoidal sinuses**:
  * 8-10 loculi in upper lateral wall of nasal cavity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Divided into anterior, middle, and posterior groups [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.3. VASCULAR SUPPLY

* **Arterial**: Upper cavity from ophthalmic artery branches (internal carotid), lower from maxillary artery and facial branches [A:40-41]
* **Little's area**: Junction of septal branches in anteroinferior septum where 90% of epistaxis occurs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Venous**: Rich submucous plexus draining to sphenopalatine, facial, and ophthalmic veins
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Communicates with cavernous sinus via ophthalmic veins, creating risk for intracranial infection spread [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.4. NASAL INTUBATION CONSIDERATIONS

* Major nasal passage lies beneath inferior concha [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Nasal tubes should be directed along floor of nose [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ Hypertrophied posterior end of inferior turbinate may cause resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ Avoid force - risk of:
  * Mucosal tears of nasal cavity/posterior pharyngeal wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Hemorrhage from posterior ethmoidal vessels (branch of internal carotid) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.5. NASAL FUNCTIONS

* **Airway**: Strong inborn reflex to breathe through nose [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Nasal obstruction causes discomfort after surgery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Choanal atresia can cause cyanosis in newborns [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Air conditioning**: Warms, humidifies, and filters inhaled air [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Reflex control**: Blood supply under autonomic regulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Warm environment → vasodilation → congestion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cold environment → vasoconstriction → clearer passages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Horner's syndrome (cervical sympathetic block) causes nasal congestion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The nose serves important functions in breathing and air conditioning, with its complex anatomy affecting nasal intubation, which should be performed along the floor of the nose to follow the main air passage while avoiding trauma to the delicate and vascular nasal structures.

## 8.3. THE PHARYNX
**Anatomical divisions of the pharynx and their significance in airway management.**

### 8.3.1. ANATOMICAL DIVISIONS

* **Nasopharynx**: Behind nasal cavity, above soft palate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Contains pharyngeal opening of Eustachian tube [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Nasopharyngeal tonsil ("adenoids") on roof/posterior wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Communicates with oropharynx through pharyngeal isthmus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Oropharynx**: From soft palate to epiglottis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Contains palatine tonsils between palatoglossal and palatopharyngeal arches [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Tonsil vascular supply primarily from tonsillar branch of facial artery [A:47-48]
* **Laryngopharynx**: From epiglottis to lower border of cricoid (C6) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Contains piriform fossae on either side of laryngeal inlet [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Common site for impaction of swallowed sharp objects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Contains internal branch of superior laryngeal nerve (can be blocked for laryngoscopy) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.3.2. STRUCTURE

* **Four layers**: Mucous, fibrous, muscular, fascial [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Mucosa: Ciliated columnar in nasopharynx, stratified squamous elsewhere [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fibrous layer: Dense above (pharyngobasilar fascia), thinner elsewhere [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Muscular coat: Constrictors and longitudinal muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fascial coat: Thin buccopharyngeal fascia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical Note**: Ludwig's angina spreads along fascial planes causing pharyngeal/laryngeal edema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

### 8.3.3. MUSCLES

* **Constrictors**: Three overlapping muscles (superior, middle, inferior) [A:49-50]
  * Inferior constrictor has two functional parts:
	* Cricopharyngeus: Transverse fibers, acts as sphincter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Thyropharyngeus: Oblique fibers, propulsive action [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ⚠️ **Clinical Pearl**: Incoordination between these components may cause pharyngeal pouch development at Killian's dehiscence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.3.4. DEGLUTITION

* Complex reflex coordinated by medullary center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Protection mechanisms prevent aspiration:
  * Nasopharynx closed by soft palate against Passavant's ridge [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Oropharyngeal isthmus narrowed by palatoglossus muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Larynx protected by:
	* Three-level sphincter mechanism (aryepiglottic folds, vestibule walls, vocal cords) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Elevation/anterior movement tucking under tongue base [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Epiglottis guiding bolus laterally through piriform fossae [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical Pearl**: Protection mechanisms impaired by:
  * Local anesthesia to pharynx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Surgical interference with pharyngeal muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Central depression from opioids, anesthesia, trauma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Anterior flap tracheostomy limiting laryngeal elevation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.3.5. AIRWAY CONSIDERATIONS

* **Airway obstruction under anesthesia**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Tongue falls backward against palate (causing snoring) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Soft palate falls back against posterior nasopharyngeal wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Relieving either obstruction will establish an airway [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Laryngeal mask airway**: Provides seal over laryngeal orifice [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Bypasses pharyngeal obstruction without need for laryngoscopy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The pharynx has three divisions (nasopharynx, oropharynx, laryngopharynx) with complex muscular and protective mechanisms that prevent aspiration during swallowing; these mechanisms are compromised by anesthesia, resulting in potential airway obstruction that can be addressed with appropriate airway management techniques.

## 8.4. THE LARYNX
**Anatomy of the larynx and its clinical significance in airway management.**

### 8.4.1. CARTILAGINOUS FRAMEWORK

* **Thyroid cartilage**: Shield-like with two laminae meeting anteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Forms Adam's apple (more prominent in males) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Has superior and inferior horns (cornua) [A:54-55]
* **Cricoid cartilage**: Signet ring-shaped [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Only complete cartilaginous ring in airway [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Narrow anteriorly, wide posterior lamina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Arytenoid cartilages**: Pyramid-shaped, sit on cricoid lamina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Have muscular process (posterior) for muscle attachments [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Have vocal process (anterior) for vocal ligament attachment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Epiglottis**: Leaf-shaped, attached to thyroid cartilage posteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Upper part projecting behind hyoid and tongue base [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower anterior surface attached to hyoid by hyo-epiglottic ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Infant epiglottis more deeply furrowed, omega-shaped on laryngoscopy [A:55-56]
* **Minor cartilages**: Corniculate (at arytenoid apex) and cuneiform (in aryepiglottic fold) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.4.2. LIGAMENTS

* **Extrinsic ligaments**:
  * Thyrohyoid membrane: Between thyroid cartilage and hyoid bone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cricotracheal ligament: Links cricoid to first tracheal ring [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cricothyroid ligament: Between thyroid and cricoid anteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* ⚠️ **Clinical Note**: Site for emergency cricothyrotomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hyo-epiglottic ligament: Connects epiglottis to hyoid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Intrinsic ligaments**:
  * Joint capsules between cartilages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Quadrangular membrane in aryepiglottic folds (forms vestibular ligament) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cricovocal membrane containing vocal ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.4.3. INTERIOR OF LARYNX

* Three regions:
  * **Vestibule**: From laryngeal inlet to vestibular folds [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Middle**: Between vestibular (false) and vocal (true) folds [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Contains sinus of larynx between folds [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Saccule extends upward from anterior part of sinus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Infraglottic cavity**: Below vocal folds to lower border of cricoid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.4.4. MUSCLES

* **Extrinsic**: Connect larynx to neighboring structures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Sternothyroid: Depresses larynx (ansa cervicalis) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Thyrohyoid: Elevates larynx (C1 via hypoglossal) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Intrinsic**: Move cartilages relative to each other [A:63-65]
  * **Posterior cricoarytenoid**: Only abductor of vocal cords [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Lateral cricoarytenoid**: Adducts cords by rotating arytenoids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Interarytenoid**: Closes posterior glottis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Thyroarytenoid**: Draws arytenoid forward, relaxes cord [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Vocalis**: Fine adjustment of cord tension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Cricothyroid**: Tensor of vocal cords [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.4.5. NERVE SUPPLY

* **Superior laryngeal nerve** (vagus branch) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * External branch: Supplies cricothyroid muscle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Internal branch: Sensory to larynx above vocal cords [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Recurrent laryngeal nerve** (vagus branch) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Motor to all intrinsic muscles except cricothyroid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Sensory below vocal cords [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right: Loops under subclavian artery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left: Loops under aortic arch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical Pearl**: Recurrent laryngeal injury can occur during thyroidectomy, particularly near inferior thyroid artery [A:67-68]

### 8.4.6. LARYNGOSCOPIC ANATOMY

* **Optimal position**: Flexion at neck, extension at atlanto-occipital joint [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Creates alignment of mouth, pharynx, and laryngeal axes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Visible structures**:
  * Base of tongue, valleculae, epiglottis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Aryepiglottic folds with cuneiform and corniculate cartilages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Vocal cords (pale and glistening) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Triangular rima glottidis opening [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Difficult intubation**: Particularly problematic with receding chin and short distance between mandibular angle and thyroid cartilage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Cormack-Lehane grading**: System for classifying laryngeal view [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Grade 1: All structures visible
  * Grade 2: Only posterior portion of glottis visible
  * Grade 3: Only epiglottis visible
  * Grade 4: No recognizable structures

**Bottom Line:** The larynx consists of a cartilaginous framework connected by ligaments and moved by intrinsic and extrinsic muscles, with dual innervation from the superior and recurrent laryngeal nerves. Understanding this anatomy is crucial for successful laryngoscopy, intubation, and managing airway complications.

## 8.5. THE TRACHEA AND MAIN BRONCHI
**Anatomy of the trachea and main bronchi and their clinical relevance to anesthesia.**

### 8.5.1. TRACHEAL ANATOMY

* **Dimensions**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Adult length: ~15cm (5cm above suprasternal notch) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Diameter: Correlates with patient's index finger [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Structure**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * 16-20 C-shaped cartilage rings [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Posterior membranous portion without cartilage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Trachealis muscle closes posterior gap [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Bifurcation** (carina): [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * At level of T4 (fixed cadaver) or T5-6 (living, erect) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Sharp sagittal ridge visible at bronchoscopy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Flattening indicates hilar lymphadenopathy or pathology [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.5.2. TRACHEAL RELATIONS

* **Cervical portion**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Anterior: Skin, fascia, isthmus of thyroid (2nd-4th rings), inferior thyroid veins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lateral: Lobes of thyroid gland, carotid sheath [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Posterior: Esophagus, recurrent laryngeal nerves in tracheoesophageal groove [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Thoracic portion**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Anterior: Thymus, origins of sternothyroid muscles, brachiocephalic and left common carotid arteries, arch of aorta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right side: Mediastinal pleura, azygos vein, right vagus nerve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left side: Left common carotid and subclavian arteries, aortic arch, left vagus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Posterior: Esophagus, with left recurrent laryngeal nerve between them [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical Pearl**: Brachiocephalic artery higher in infants, may be injured during tracheostomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.5.3. MAIN BRONCHI

* **Right main bronchus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Shorter (2.5cm), wider, and more vertical (25° from vertical) than left [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Gives off right upper lobe bronchus after 2.5cm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: More likely path for aspiration or bronchial intubation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Left main bronchus**: [A:76-77] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Longer (5cm), narrower, more horizontal (45° from vertical) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Passes under aortic arch, in front of esophagus/thoracic duct/descending aorta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.5.4. ENDOBRONCHIAL TUBES

* **Right-sided challenges**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Right upper lobe bronchus arises close to carina [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Special tubes with lateral opening needed to ventilate RUL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Left-sided advantages**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * 5cm distance between carina and left upper lobe bronchus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Allows standard tube placement without upper lobe obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Position should be confirmed by fiberoptic bronchoscopy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.5.5. CLINICAL PROCEDURES

* **Tracheostomy**: [A:72-73] [B:21-22]
  * Classic surgical approach: Transverse incision midway between cricoid and suprasternal notch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Emergency approach: Vertical incision from lower thyroid border to just above suprasternal notch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Key principle: Stay in midline to avoid major vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ⚠️ Complications: Hemorrhage, tracheal erosion, pneumothorax [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Percutaneous tracheostomy**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Seldinger wire-guided technique between 1st and 2nd tracheal rings [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Progressive dilation with dilators or balloon [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Preferred technique in many ICUs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Cricothyrotomy**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Through cricothyroid membrane for emergency airway access [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Horizontal incision over membrane with scalpel rotation to enlarge [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Less vascular than full tracheostomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The trachea extends from the cricoid cartilage to the carina (T4-T6), with the right main bronchus being shorter, wider, and more vertical than the left, explaining the predisposition for right mainstem intubation and aspiration. Understanding these relationships is essential for airway management, including tracheostomy and endobronchial tube placement.

## 8.6. THE PLEURA
**Anatomy of the pleural membranes and their relevance to respiratory medicine and procedures.**

### 8.6.1. ANATOMY AND ARRANGEMENT

* **Definition**: Double-walled serous sac investing each lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Visceral layer: Invests lung, dips into fissures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Parietal layer: Lines chest wall, diaphragm, mediastinum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Meet at hilum, forming pulmonary ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pleural cavity**: Potential space between layers [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Contains thin film of serous fluid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Visceral pleura tightly adherent to lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Parietal pleura can be stripped from chest wall via extrapleural fascia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.6.2. PLEURAL REFLECTIONS

* **Apex**: Extends 4cm above medial third of clavicle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Anterior border**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Right: Meets opposite pleura at sternal angle (level of 2nd costal cartilage) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left: Deviates laterally at 4th cartilage (cardiac notch) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Lower border**: [A:78-79] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * 8th rib at mid-clavicular line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 10th rib at mid-axillary line (lowest point) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 12th rib at paravertebral line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical Note**: Pleura doesn't extend to diaphragmatic attachment line, creating zone where chest wall directly contacts diaphragm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.6.3. COSTO-DIAPHRAGMATIC RECESS

* Space between costal and diaphragmatic pleura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Not filled by lung during quiet respiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reserve space filled during deep inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ May be inadvertently entered during operations below 12th rib [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.6.4. CHEST TUBE INSERTION

* **Safe triangle**: [A:89-90] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Bordered by anterior border of latissimus dorsi, lateral border of pectoralis major, horizontal line at 5th intercostal space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Position arm behind head to expose area [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Technique**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [B:22-23]
  * Use ultrasound guidance when possible for fluid drainage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Infiltrate local anesthetic through intercostal space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Make small incision in skin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Blunt dissection over upper border of rib to avoid neurovascular bundle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Insert finger to clear lung before tube placement [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ Always confirm position with chest X-ray [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The pleura forms a double-layered membrane around each lung, with reflections that define the limits of the thoracic cavity. Knowledge of pleural surface markings and the safe triangle for chest tube insertion is essential for performing procedures like thoracentesis and chest tube placement while avoiding iatrogenic injury.

## 8.7. THE INTERCOSTAL SPACES
**Anatomy of the intercostal spaces and its clinical applications.**

### 8.7.1. STRUCTURE AND COMPOSITION

* **Muscular layers**: Correspond to three layers of abdominal wall [A:91-92] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * External intercostals: Downward and forward from rib to rib below, anterior intercostal membrane beyond costal cartilages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Internal intercostals: Downward and backward, posterior intercostal membrane beyond angles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Innermost layer: Transversus thoracis (anterior), intercostales intimi (lateral), subcostals (posterior) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Endothoracic fascia**: Fine areolar layer between muscles and parietal pleura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.7.2. RESPIRATORY FUNCTION

* Intercostal muscles assist with breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Contract during inspiration, lifting ribs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Lower intercostal muscles also contract during forcible expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Paralysis decreases rib movement amplitude [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Rigid fixation (e.g., ankylosing spondylitis) reduces breathing capacity by 20-30% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.7.3. NEUROVASCULAR BUNDLE

* Location: Runs in costal groove of upper rib [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* Components (top to bottom): [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Posterior intercostal vein
  * Posterior intercostal artery
  * Intercostal nerve
* **Course**: [A:93-94]
  * Posteriorly: Between pleura and posterior intercostal membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * At rib angle: Passes between internal intercostal and intracostal muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.7.4. BLOOD VESSELS

* **Posterior intercostal arteries**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 3rd-11th spaces: Direct from thoracic aorta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 1st-2nd spaces: From superior intercostal artery (costocervical trunk) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Anterior intercostal arteries**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 1st-6th spaces: From internal thoracic artery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 7th-9th spaces: From musculophrenic branch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Intercostal veins**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Variable drainage patterns [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Upper veins to brachiocephalic via superior intercostal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower veins to azygos/hemiazygos system [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.7.5. NERVES

* **Intercostal nerves**: Anterior primary rami of T1-11 [A:94-95] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Branches**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Collateral: Motor branch at rib angle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lateral cutaneous: At mid-axillary line, divides into anterior/posterior branches [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Anterior cutaneous: Terminal branch at sternum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Special characteristics**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * T1: Primarily contributes to brachial plexus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * T2: Lateral cutaneous branch forms intercostobrachial nerve (medial arm sensation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Not affected by brachial plexus blocks, needs separate block for tourniquet pain [A:95-96]
  * T7-11: Continue into abdominal wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.7.6. INTERCOSTAL NERVE BLOCK

* **Indications**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Postoperative analgesia for thoracic/upper abdominal surgery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pain from fractured ribs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Chronic pain conditions (e.g., post-herpetic neuralgia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Technique**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Patient sitting, lateral, or prone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Block at angle of rib [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Walk needle off lower border of rib [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Advance 3-5mm in slightly cephalad direction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inject 3-5ml local anesthetic after negative aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Caution**: Total dose limited due to rapid vascular absorption [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The intercostal spaces contain three muscle layers and a neurovascular bundle that runs in the costal groove of the upper rib, protected from procedures performed just above the lower rib. Understanding this anatomy is crucial for intercostal nerve blocks and avoiding neurovascular injury during thoracic procedures.

## 8.8. THE LUNGS
**Detailed anatomy of the lungs, their lobes, segments, and vascular supply.**

### 8.8.1. GROSS ANATOMY

* **Shape and location**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Conical organs filling pleural cavities [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right lung larger/heavier (620g vs 570g) but shorter due to higher diaphragm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Surfaces and borders**: [A:97-99] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Apex: Extends 4cm above medial third of clavicle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Base: Concave, rests on diaphragm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Costal surface: Relates to ribcage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Medial surface: Contains hilum, relates to mediastinal structures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Right: Contacts right atrium/ventricle, SVC/IVC, azygos vein, trachea, vagus nerve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Left: Contacts left auricle/ventricle, aortic arch, subclavian/carotid arteries, trachea, esophagus, vagus nerve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Anterior borders: Thin edges between pleura and chest wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Left has cardiac notch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.8.2. LUNG LOBES AND FISSURES

* **Right lung**: Three lobes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Divided by oblique and horizontal fissures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Oblique fissure: From T5 posteriorly to 6th rib/space anteriorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Horizontal fissure: From 4th costal cartilage to 5th rib at midaxillary line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Left lung**: Two lobes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Divided by oblique fissure only [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lingula corresponds anatomically to right middle lobe [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Scapular border with arm raised corresponds to oblique fissure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.8.3. HILAR RELATIONSHIPS

* **Components**: Pulmonary artery, pulmonary veins, bronchus, bronchial vessels, lymphatics, nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Arrangement**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Bronchi posterior to vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Arteries superior to veins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right: Upper lobe bronchus above ("eparterial") but still posterior to vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left: All branches "hyparterial" [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Hilar relations**: [A:102-103]
  * Right: SVC and phrenic nerve anteriorly, vagus posteriorly, azygos vein superiorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left: Phrenic nerve anteriorly, descending aorta and vagus posteriorly, aortic arch superiorly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.8.4. BRONCHOPULMONARY SEGMENTS

* **Definition**: Functional lung units with own bronchus, artery, and distinct parenchyma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Right lung segments**: [A:103-104]
  * Upper lobe: Apical (1), Posterior (2), Anterior (3) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Middle lobe: Lateral (4), Medial (5) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower lobe: Apical (6), Medial basal/Cardiac (7), Anterior basal (8), Lateral basal (9), Posterior basal (10) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Left lung segments**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Upper lobe: Apicoposterior (1+2), Anterior (3) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lingula: Superior (4), Inferior (5) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower lobe: Apical (6), Anterior basal (8), Lateral basal (9), Posterior basal (10) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* No separate medial basal segment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.8.5. BRONCHOSCOPIC ANATOMY

* **Trachea** appears as white rings with reddish membranous portion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Carina**: Sharp sagittal ridge, slightly left of midline [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Right main bronchus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Wider, more vertical, easier to enter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right upper lobe bronchus visible on lateral wall with tricornate appearance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Middle lobe appears as horizontal ridge [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Left main bronchus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Longer with upper lobe bronchus dividing into lingular and proper upper lobe branches [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.8.6. MICROSCOPIC ANATOMY

* **Airway wall structure**: [A:107-108]
  * Mucosa: Ciliated columnar epithelium with goblet cells in larger airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Submucosa: Longitudinal elastic fibers, vascular plexus, lymphoid tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Muscle: Non-striated arranged in geodesic network [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cartilage: Rings in main bronchi to irregular plates in branches, absent in bronchioles <0.6mm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Terminal air spaces**: [A:108-109] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Bronchioles (~0.6mm): No cartilage, cuboidal ciliated epithelium [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Respiratory bronchioles: Bear alveoli on walls, non-ciliated epithelium [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Alveolar ducts, atria, air sacs, air cells (alveoli) [A:108-109]

### 8.8.7. PULMONARY VASCULATURE

* **Pulmonary circulation**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary artery: Follows bronchial tree, carries deoxygenated blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary veins: Run in intersegmental planes, convenient landmarks for segmental resection [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchial circulation**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Supplies bronchi, lung tissue, pleura and nodes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Usually three arteries (one right, two left) of variable origin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Distal bronchial arterial blood drains into pulmonary veins (physiologic shunt) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The lungs are divided into lobes by fissures and further into bronchopulmonary segments, each with its own bronchus, artery, and venous drainage. Understanding this segmental anatomy is essential for bronchoscopy, lung isolation techniques, and interpreting imaging studies in respiratory medicine.

## 8.9. THE DIAPHRAGM
**Anatomy and function of the diaphragm in respiration and other physiological processes.**

### 8.9.1. ANATOMICAL FEATURES

* **Definition**: Musculotendinous septum between thorax and abdomen [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
* **Structure**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Peripheral muscle with central trefoil tendon (blends with pericardium) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Origins: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Crura: From lumbar vertebrae (left from L1-2, right from L1-3) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Arcuate ligaments: Median, medial (over psoas), lateral (over quadratus lumborum) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Costal origin: From tips of last six costal cartilages [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Xiphoid origin: Two slips from posterior xiphoid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.9.2. DIAPHRAGMATIC FORAMINA

* **Three major openings**: [A:112-113] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Inferior vena cava: At T8 level [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Esophagus: At T10 with vagal trunks and esophageal branches of left gastric vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Reinforced by muscular sling from right crus (important for GE junction competence) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Aorta: Behind median arcuate ligament at T12 with thoracic duct and azygos vein [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Additional structures passing through diaphragm**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Sympathetic trunk: Behind medial arcuate ligament [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Splanchnic nerves: Through crura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hemiazygos vein: Through left crus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Superior epigastric vessels: Between xiphoid and costal origins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower intercostal nerves and vessels: Between diaphragm and transversus abdominis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lymphatics: From retroperitoneum to mediastinum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.9.3. NERVE SUPPLY

* **Phrenic nerve (C3-5)**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Primary motor supply to diaphragm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right pierces central tendon lateral to IVC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Left pierces muscle ~1cm lateral to pericardial attachment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Terminal branches supply abdominal aspect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Sensory supply**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Central portion: Proprioceptive fibers via phrenic nerve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Periphery: Lower thoracic nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.9.4. RESPIRATORY FUNCTION

* **Position**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [B:24-25]
  * Dome reaches level of 5th rib at midclavicular line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right hemidiaphragm higher than left [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Both elevated in late pregnancy, ascites, obesity, large tumors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Breathing mechanics**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Inspiration: Diaphragm descends, creating negative intrathoracic pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Expiration: Passive relaxation in normal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Forced expiration: Pushed upward by abdominal muscle contraction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Accounts for 60-75% of tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Movement range: 1.5cm quiet breathing, 7-13cm deep breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 💡 **Clinical Pearl**: Bilateral phrenic interruption may cause little respiratory difficulty if lungs normal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.9.5. GASTROINTESTINAL FUNCTION

* **Gastroesophageal junction**: [A:115-116] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Diaphragm contributes to "cardiac sphincter" mechanism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Components of antireflux barrier: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Physiological muscular sphincter at lower esophagus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Mucosal fold plug at cardia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Oblique angle of gastroesophageal junction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Diaphragmatic sling pinch-cock effect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Positive intra-abdominal pressure compressing intra-abdominal esophagus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ⚠️ Hiatal hernia compromises this mechanism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 8.9.6. DEVELOPMENT AND ANOMALIES

* **Formation**: Fusion of four components [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Septum transversum: Forms central tendon [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Dorsal esophageal mesentery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Peripheral rim from body wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pleuroperitoneal membranes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Congenital defects**: [A:117-118] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Foramen of Morgagni: Anterior between xiphoid and costal origins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Foramen of Bochdalek: Posterolateral (pleuroperitoneal canal) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Central tendon deficiency [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Congenitally large esophageal hiatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Acquired hiatus herniae**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|ⓘ]]
  * Sliding hernia: Stomach/lower esophagus slide upward through lax hiatus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Rolling/paraesophageal hernia: Fundus rolls up through hiatus while cardia remains in position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The diaphragm is the principal muscle of respiration, accounting for 60-75% of tidal volume. It contains foramina for major structures and contributes to the gastroesophageal reflux barrier. Congenital or acquired defects can lead to herniation, affecting both respiratory and digestive functions.

# 9. VENTILATION-PERFUSION RELATIONSHIPS





## 9.1. EFFECTS OF GRAVITY ON BLOOD FLOW

**How gravitational forces affect blood flow distribution in the lungs.**

* Blood flow increases linearly from top to bottom of lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Difference due to hydrostatic pressure gradient in pulmonary capillaries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pressure difference from apex to base approximately 22 mmHg (30 cm height) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Significant impact on low-pressure pulmonary circulation (MPAP typically 15 mmHg) [A:68-69]

**Regional blood flow modifications**:
* **Exercise**: Increased cardiac output raises MPAP, reducing relative importance of hydrostatic gradient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Body position**:
  * Supine: Anterior lung becomes "apex," posterior becomes "base" [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lateral: Dependent (lower) lung receives greater perfusion than non-dependent (upper) lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Gravity creates a vertical gradient of pulmonary perfusion, with blood flow increasing from apex to base in the upright position, though this effect is modified by exercise, body position, and non-gravitational factors like vascular architecture.

## 9.2. EFFECTS OF GRAVITY ON ALVEOLAR VENTILATION

**How gravitational forces affect distribution of air within the lungs.**

* Ventilation increases from apex to base in upright lung, but less dramatically than perfusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Distribution determined by pleural pressure gradient and compliance differences [A:69-70]
* Pleural pressure more negative at apex (-10 cmH2O) than base (-2.5 cmH2O) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* At FRC, basal alveoli have greater compliance than apical alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* With inspiration, basal alveoli expand more due to greater compliance (having a relatively more positive pleural pressure means that it is less expanded at rest, thus it can expand more during inspiration) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in progressively increasing ventilation from apex to base [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![ventilation_gradient.png](ventilation_gradient.png)

**Bottom Line:** Gravity affects ventilation through a pleural pressure gradient that creates regional compliance differences, with greater ventilation in dependent lung regions, but the ventilation gradient is less steep than the perfusion gradient.

## 9.3. VENTILATION-PERFUSION RATIO

**The relationship between ventilation and perfusion and its distribution throughout the lung.**

* **Definition**: Ratio of alveolar ventilation to perfusion for a region of lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Normal overall V/Q ratio**: Approximately 0.8 (ventilation 4 L/min, perfusion 5 L/min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Regional variation**:
  * Base: V/Q ≈ 0.6 (lower ratio - perfusion exceeds ventilation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Apex: V/Q ≈ 3.0 (higher ratio - ventilation exceeds perfusion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![vq_ratio_zones.png](vq_ratio_zones.png)
* **Apex characteristics**:
  * High V/Q results in fully oxygenated blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Higher PAO2, lower PACO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Minimal gas exchange due to limited blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Base characteristics**:
  * Low V/Q may result in incomplete oxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lower PAO2, higher PACO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * More gas exchange occurs due to greater blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** V/Q ratio varies with lung height, increasing from base to apex due to gravity's differential effects on ventilation and perfusion. This creates regional differences in gas exchange efficiency, with potential for right-to-left shunt in dependent regions and wasted ventilation in non-dependent regions.

## 9.4. CAUSES OF ABNORMAL V/Q RATIO

**Pathological conditions that disrupt normal ventilation-perfusion matching.**

**Low V/Q Ratio (Decreased ventilation relative to perfusion)**:
* Upper airway obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Foreign body aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pneumonia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pneumothorax [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ARDS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Emphysema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* One-lung ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Normal aging [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased closing capacity (e.g., in obesity) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**High V/Q Ratio (Decreased perfusion relative to ventilation)**:
* Pulmonary embolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reduced right ventricular stroke volume (hypovolemia, right ventricular infarction, tamponade) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Abnormal V/Q ratios result from conditions that either impair regional ventilation (creating low V/Q) or reduce regional perfusion (creating high V/Q), with low V/Q being the more common cause of hypoxemia.

## 9.5. MANAGEMENT OF V/Q MISMATCH

**Therapeutic approaches to address ventilation-perfusion inequality.**

**Low V/Q Management**:
* Administer supplemental oxygen - increasing FiO2 raises PAO2 and improves oxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Target underlying cause [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]:
  * Antibiotics for pneumonia
  * Bronchodilators for bronchospasm
  * PEEP for atelectasis
  * Chest drain for pneumothorax
  * Removal of foreign body if present

**High V/Q Management**:
* Address underlying cause [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]:
  * Anticoagulation and supportive care for pulmonary embolism
  * Volume resuscitation for hypovolemia
  * Improve right ventricular function in right heart failure

**Special Considerations for Pulmonary Embolism**:
* Despite high V/Q regions, patients become hypoxemic due to [A:71-72]:
  * Local inflammatory response causing bronchospasm
  * Surfactant reduction causing atelectasis
  * Inflammatory mediators causing pulmonary edema
  * These mechanisms create areas of low V/Q or shunt, explaining paradoxical hypoxemia

**Bottom Line:** Management of V/Q mismatch focuses on raising oxygen tension in low V/Q areas through supplemental oxygen while addressing the underlying cause of the mismatch. Even conditions primarily creating high V/Q areas (like PE) often cause hypoxemia through secondary mechanisms creating low V/Q regions.

## 9.6. OTHER FACTORS INFLUENCING V/Q MATCHING

**Non-gravitational mechanisms affecting ventilation-perfusion matching.**

* **Airway-vessel partnership**: Airways and blood vessels develop together embryologically, resulting in matched branching patterns and diameters [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction**:
  * Physiological response to alveolar hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary arterioles constrict when supplying poorly ventilated alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Diverts blood to better ventilated regions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Helps normalize V/Q ratios [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Impaired by inhalational anesthetics, sepsis, vasodilators [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Capillary recruitment and distension**:
  * Well-ventilated regions accommodate increased blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Previously closed capillaries open (recruitment) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Open capillaries widen (distension) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Helps maintain normal V/Q despite regional perfusion changes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Beyond gravity, V/Q matching depends on structural airway-vessel relationships, hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction that diverts blood from poorly ventilated regions, and capillary recruitment/distension that accommodates blood flow in well-ventilated areas.

# 10. VENTILATION-PERFUSION ZONES IN THE LUNG





## 10.1. WEST ZONES OF THE LUNG

**Division of upright lung into zones based on relationship between alveolar, arterial, and venous pressures.**

* Conceptual model dividing lung into four zones based on blood flow dynamics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Based on relationship between three pressures [A:71-72]:

![West Zones](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_West_zones__VQ.001.jpeg_e2677_.jpeg)
  * Pa = pulmonary arterial pressure
  * PA = alveolar pressure
  * Pv = pulmonary venous pressure
* Zones numbered 1-4 from apex to base [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** West zones provide a framework for understanding regional differences in pulmonary blood flow based on the relationship between vascular pressures and alveolar pressure, which varies with vertical height in the lung.

## 10.2. EFFECTS ON ALVEOLAR PERFUSION

**How pressure relationships in different lung zones affect capillary blood flow.**

**Zone 1 (PA > Pa > Pv)**:
* Alveolar pressure exceeds both arterial and venous pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Compresses pulmonary capillaries, preventing blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Creates alveolar dead space (ventilation without perfusion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Not present in normal lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Can occur with hypovolemia or high PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Zone 2 (Pa > PA > Pv)**:
* Arterial pressure exceeds alveolar pressure, but venous pressure lower than alveolar [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Creates a "waterfall" effect - flow determined by arterial-alveolar pressure difference [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Blood flow varies through cardiac cycle (higher in systole) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
