* Flow increases down this zone as arterial pressure increases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Typically exists between apex and mid-lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Zone 3 (Pa > Pv > PA)**:
* Both arterial and venous pressures exceed alveolar pressure [A:72-73]
* Flow determined by arterial-venous pressure difference [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Flow continuous throughout cardiac cycle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Flow increases down this zone due to increasing hydrostatic pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Typically comprises most of the lung below mid-level [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Zone 4 (Extra zone added later to West's model)**:
* Similar to Zone 3 but with increased resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Occurs at lung base where interstitial pressure may compress extra-alveolar vessels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* May slightly reduce blood flow at extreme base [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary blood flow patterns vary by lung zone - no flow in Zone 1 (where PA exceeds vascular pressures), flow determined by arterial-alveolar pressure difference in Zone 2, and by arterial-venous pressure difference in Zone 3, with Zone 4 showing increased resistance at the extreme base.

## 10.3. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE OF WEST ZONE 1

**Conditions creating Zone 1 physiology and their clinical implications.**

**Conditions creating Zone 1**:
* Pulmonary arterial pressure abnormally low (e.g., hypovolemic shock) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Alveolar pressure abnormally high:
  * Positive pressure ventilation with high airway pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * High intrinsic PEEP (e.g., severe asthma) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Clinical consequences**:
* Increased alveolar dead space [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Inefficient ventilation (wasted ventilatory effort) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Potential respiratory failure in severe asthma (exhaustion from high work of breathing) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** West Zone 1 represents areas where capillaries are collapsed by alveolar pressure exceeding vascular pressures, creating inefficient ventilation. While not normally present, Zone 1 can develop with low pulmonary arterial pressure or high alveolar pressure, potentially contributing to respiratory failure.

## 10.4. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS - PULMONARY ARTERY CATHETERS

**Use of West zone physiology in pulmonary artery catheter measurements.**

* Pulmonary artery catheters (PACs) provide hemodynamic measurements including pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (PCWP) [A:72-73]
* PCWP approximates left atrial pressure, an indicator of left ventricular filling pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **PAC wedge position requirements**:
  * Catheter must be in West Zone 3 for accurate PCWP measurement [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Requires uninterrupted static column of blood between wedged catheter and left atrium [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Only occurs when Pv > PA (i.e., Zone 3) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical verification of correct zone**:
  * Most pulmonary blood flow directed to Zone 3, so flow-directed catheters typically position correctly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Test: If increasing PEEP by 10 cmH2O causes PCWP to increase by >25%, the catheter tip is likely not in Zone 3 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary artery catheters must be positioned in West Zone 3 (where venous pressure exceeds alveolar pressure) to accurately measure PCWP. Verification is important as catheter placement in Zones 1 or 2 will give inaccurate readings that could lead to inappropriate clinical decisions.

# 11. OXYGEN DELIVERY AND DEMAND





## 11.1. GLOBAL OXYGEN CONSUMPTION

**The volume of oxygen consumed by tissues per minute to support metabolism.**

* **Definition**: Volume of O2 consumed by the body per minute (V̇O2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Normal value**: Approximately 250 mL/min at rest (70-kg adult) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Measurement**: Calculated using reverse Fick principle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V̇O2 = CO × (CaO2 - CvO2) × 10 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CO = cardiac output (L/min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CaO2 = arterial oxygen content (mL O2/100 mL blood) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CvO2 = venous oxygen content (mL O2/100 mL blood) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 10 = conversion factor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical significance**:
  * Closely matches metabolic rate during aerobic metabolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increases with exercise until V̇O2max is reached [A:74-75]
  * V̇O2max represents transition point to anaerobic metabolism and limits performance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Global oxygen consumption represents the total oxygen used by tissues for metabolism, typically 250 mL/min at rest, which can be calculated using the reverse Fick principle by measuring the arteriovenous oxygen content difference and cardiac output.

## 11.2. GLOBAL OXYGEN DELIVERY

**The volume of oxygen transported from lungs to tissues per minute.**

* **Definition**: Volume of O2 delivered to tissues per minute (ḊO2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Calculation**: Using the oxygen flux equation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ḊO2 = CO × CaO2 × 10 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CO = cardiac output (L/min) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CaO2 = arterial oxygen content (mL O2/100 mL blood) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 10 = conversion factor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Components of arterial oxygen content** (CaO2):
  * CaO2 = (1.34 × [Hb] × SaO2/100%) + (0.023 × PaO2) [A:74-75]
  * [Hb] = hemoglobin concentration (g/dL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * SaO2 = hemoglobin oxygen saturation (%) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PaO2 = arterial oxygen tension (kPa) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 1.34 = volume of O2 (mL) carried by 1g of fully saturated hemoglobin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 0.023 = solubility coefficient of O2 in plasma (mL/dL/kPa) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Oxygen delivery represents the total oxygen transported from lungs to tissues, calculated as the product of cardiac output and arterial oxygen content, with oxygen carried primarily bound to hemoglobin and a small amount dissolved in plasma.

## 11.3. TYPICAL RESTING OXYGEN DELIVERY

**Normal values for oxygen delivery and its components in healthy individuals.**

* **Normal ḊO2**: Approximately 1000 mL/min in a resting adult [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Calculation example** using typical values:
  * CO = 5 L/min [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * [Hb] = 15 g/dL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * SaO2 = 98% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PaO2 = 13 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CaO2 = (1.34 × 15 × 98/100) + (0.023 × 13) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CaO2 = 19.7 + 0.3 = 20 mL O2/100 mL blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ḊO2 = 5 × 20 × 10 = 1000 mL O2/min [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Effects of anemia**:
  * Reduced [Hb] decreases CaO2 (e.g., [Hb] of 8 g/dL reduces CaO2 to ~10.8 mL O2/100 mL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Compensatory increase in CO helps maintain ḊO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Decreased blood viscosity contributes to improved flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Normal oxygen delivery is approximately 1000 mL/min at rest, far exceeding the typical oxygen consumption of 250 mL/min. Anemia reduces oxygen-carrying capacity, but compensatory mechanisms like increased cardiac output help maintain adequate oxygen delivery.

## 11.4. OXYGEN EXTRACTION RATIO

**The proportion of delivered oxygen extracted by tissues for metabolism.**

* **Definition**: Fraction of delivered oxygen consumed by tissues [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Calculation**:
  * OER = V̇O2/ḊO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Normal value: 0.2-0.3 (20-30%) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Relationship to venous saturation**:
  * OER of 0.2-0.3 corresponds to mixed venous saturation (SvO2) of 70-80% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Organ-specific variation**:
  * Carotid bodies: Low OER [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Heart: High OER (~0.6), makes it susceptible to ischemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Adaptive responses**:
  * As ḊO2 falls or V̇O2 rises, OER increases to maintain aerobic metabolism [A:75-76]
  * Tissues extract more oxygen from passing blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * SvO2 decreases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The oxygen extraction ratio represents the proportion of delivered oxygen used by tissues, normally 20-30%. This ratio varies between organs and increases when delivery falls or demand rises, representing an important adaptive mechanism to maintain aerobic metabolism.

## 11.5. CRITICAL OXYGEN DELIVERY

**The threshold of oxygen delivery below which aerobic metabolism cannot be maintained.**

* **Definition**: Minimum oxygen delivery required to meet resting metabolic needs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Critical DO2](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_Critical_DO2.001.jpeg_3a1ec0._.jpeg)
* **Normal value**: Approximately 4-8 mL/kg/min [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Supply dependency**:
  * Above critical ḊO2: Oxygen consumption is supply-independent (stable despite ḊO2 changes) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Below critical ḊO2: Oxygen consumption becomes supply-dependent (falls as ḊO2 falls) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Threshold is called the anaerobic threshold [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Consequences below threshold**:
  * Insufficient oxygen for aerobic metabolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Shift to anaerobic metabolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increasing lactate production [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Metabolic acidosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cellular dysfunction and eventually organ failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Critical oxygen delivery represents the threshold below which tissues cannot extract enough oxygen to meet metabolic demands, forcing a shift to anaerobic metabolism with resulting lactate production and acidosis. Maintaining oxygen delivery above this threshold is essential for preventing tissue hypoxia and organ dysfunction.

## 11.6. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS IN CRITICAL ILLNESS AND ANESTHESIA

**Oxygen supply and demand considerations in critical care and perioperative settings.**

**Critical Illness Considerations**:
* Altered oxygen demand in critically ill patients:
  * Increased by inflammation, sepsis, fever [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased by adrenergic drugs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased during ventilator weaning [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased with physical interventions (e.g., physiotherapy) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased in trauma, burns, seizures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Supply dependency may occur at higher thresholds in sepsis and ARDS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Controversial whether maximizing ḊO2 improves outcomes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Perioperative Considerations**:
* Increased oxygen consumption:
  * Surgical stress [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pain and anxiety [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inflammation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Post-operative shivering [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Factors reducing oxygen consumption during anesthesia:
  * Sedatives/hypnotics (reduce cerebral metabolic rate) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Mechanical ventilation (eliminates work of breathing) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Muscle paralysis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Preoperative fasting (reduces gut O2 consumption) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Post-surgical inflammatory response increases V̇O2 by ~50% for several days [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Preoperative assessment of cardiopulmonary reserve essential (e.g., cardiopulmonary exercise testing) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Critical illness and surgery both alter oxygen supply-demand balance. In critical illness, increased metabolic demands and altered physiology may raise the critical delivery threshold. In the perioperative period, anesthesia may transiently reduce consumption, but surgical stress and inflammation ultimately increase oxygen requirements, necessitating adequate cardiopulmonary reserve to meet postoperative demands.

# 12. OXYGEN CASCADE




## 12.1. DEFINITION AND STEPS

**The step-wise reduction in oxygen tension from atmosphere to mitochondria.**

### 12.1.1. CONCEPT AND PURPOSE

* **Definition**: The oxygen cascade refers to the stepwise reduction in PO2 as O2 passes from the environment to the tissues [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Purpose**: Enables efficient delivery of O2 from high atmospheric concentration to cellular mitochondria for aerobic metabolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

![Oxygen Cascade](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_O2_cascade.001.jpeg_69ac81.jp_.jpeg)

### 12.1.2. CASCADE STEPS

* **Atmosphere**: PO2 = 21.2 kPa (101.325 kPa × 20.93% O2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Atmospheric pressure at sea level is 101.325 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fraction of O2 in dry air is 20.93% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Trachea**: PO2 = 19.9 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduction due to humidification [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inspired air becomes fully saturated with water vapor (PSVP water = 6.3 kPa at 37°C) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PO2 = (101.325 - 6.3) × 20.93% = 19.9 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Alveolus**: PO2 = 13.3 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Determined by alveolar gas equation: PAO2 = FiO2(PB - PH2O) - (PaCO2/R) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Using typical values: PaCO2 = 5.3 kPa, R = 0.8 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PAO2 = 20.93%(101.325 - 6.3) - 5.3/0.8 = 13.3 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Arterial Blood**: PO2 = 13.0 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Small drop (0.5-1.0 kPa) from alveolar PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduction from diffusion barrier and physiological shunting [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Normal A-a gradient is 0.5-1.0 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Tissue Capillaries**: Progressive reduction in PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Falls along capillary length as O2 diffuses to tissues [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PO2 gradient varies by tissue metabolic activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Mixed venous PO2 typically 5.3 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Mitochondria**: PO2 ≈ 1.5 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Final site of oxygen utilization in electron transport chain [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Minimum PO2 for oxidative phosphorylation (Pasteur point) ≈ 0.13 kPa (1 mmHg) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The oxygen cascade describes how oxygen tension progressively falls as O2 moves from atmosphere (21.2 kPa) to mitochondria (~1.5 kPa), with reductions at each step due to humidification, alveolar gas mixing, diffusion barriers, and oxygen consumption by tissues.

## 12.2. PASTEUR POINT AND ANAEROBIC METABOLISM

**The critical threshold for oxygen-dependent cellular metabolism.**

### 12.2.1. DEFINITION AND SIGNIFICANCE

* **Pasteur Point**: Minimum oxygen tension required for aerobic metabolism (0.13-0.30 kPa) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* When mitochondrial PO2 falls below this threshold, cells switch to anaerobic metabolism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Anaerobic metabolism becomes dominant energy production pathway [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 12.2.2. CAUSES OF CASCADE DISRUPTION

* **High Altitude**: Reduced atmospheric PO2 affects all subsequent steps [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * With decreasing barometric pressure, all PO2 levels in the cascade decrease proportionally [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * At 8848m (Mount Everest), atmospheric pressure ≈ 34 kPa, resulting in PO2 ≈ 7 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Hypoventilation**: Increases PaCO2, which reduces PAO2 as per alveolar gas equation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Common with respiratory depression (opioids, anesthesia, neurological injury) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **V/Q Mismatch**: Leads to regional differences in alveolar PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Particularly important in conditions like pneumonia and ARDS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Gas Transfer Impairment**: Widens A-a gradient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Seen in interstitial lung disease, pulmonary edema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Circulatory Failure**: Impairs O2 delivery despite normal PaO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Shock states reduce tissue perfusion and increase O2 extraction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in lower capillary and mitochondrial PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 12.2.3. CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS AND MANAGEMENT

* **Tissue Hypoxia Signs**:
  * Metabolic acidosis (elevated lactate from anaerobic metabolism) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Organ dysfunction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cellular death if prolonged [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Management Approach**:
  * Identify and address which step of the cascade is compromised [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Supplemental oxygen addresses atmospheric/alveolar steps [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Ventilatory support treats hypoventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Circulatory support improves oxygen delivery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The Pasteur point represents the critical oxygen tension threshold (0.13-0.30 kPa) below which aerobic metabolism fails and cells switch to less efficient anaerobic pathways. Disruptions anywhere along the oxygen cascade can ultimately lead to mitochondrial hypoxia, with management focusing on identifying and correcting the specific step affected.

# 13. LUNG COMPLIANCE




## 13.1. DEFINITION AND DETERMINANTS

**The relationship between pressure change and volume change in the respiratory system.**

### 13.1.1. BASIC CONCEPT

* Compliance is the change in lung volume produced by a unit change in transpulmonary pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Mathematically: Compliance = ΔVolume/ΔTranspulmonary pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Essential property determining how easily lungs expand when pressure is applied [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.1.2. EFFECTS ON BREATHING

* **High Compliance**:
  * Small transpulmonary pressure needed to achieve tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Less work required for inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Less elastic recoil stored, making passive exhalation more difficult [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Low Compliance**:
  * Large transpulmonary pressure needed to achieve same tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * May lead to respiratory failure from muscle fatigue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.1.3. NORMAL VALUES

* At functional residual capacity (FRC), typical value is 200 mL/cmH2O [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* A tidal volume of 500 mL requires approximately 2.5 cmH2O of transpulmonary pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Lung compliance describes how easily the lungs expand in response to pressure changes, with high compliance requiring less work for inspiration but providing less elastic recoil for expiration, and low compliance increasing the work of breathing.

## 13.2. RESPIRATORY COMPLIANCE

**The combined compliance of the lungs and chest wall.**

### 13.2.1. COMPONENTS

* Respiratory compliance (RC) includes both:
  * Lung compliance (LC) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Thoracic cage compliance (TCC) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.2.2. MATHEMATICAL RELATIONSHIP

* Compliances in series add as reciprocals: 1/RC = 1/LC + 1/TCC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* With typical values of 200 mL/cmH2O for both lung and thoracic cage compliance, respiratory compliance is 100 mL/cmH2O [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* The overall compliance is always less than the sum of its parts [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.2.3. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* Changes in either lung or chest wall compliance affect overall respiratory mechanics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Treatment approaches differ based on whether lung or chest wall compliance is primarily affected [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Respiratory compliance represents the combined effect of lung and chest wall compliances, which act in series, with overall compliance always less than either individual component.

## 13.3. FACTORS AFFECTING COMPLIANCE

**Conditions and states that alter respiratory system compliance.**

### 13.3.1. FACTORS INCREASING COMPLIANCE

* **Emphysema**: Destruction of elastic tissue in lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Advancing age**: Progressive degeneration of elastic tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Neuromuscular conditions**: Decreased muscle tone allows easier chest wall expansion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.3.2. FACTORS DECREASING COMPLIANCE

* **Posture**: Compliance lower when supine compared to upright [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pregnancy**: Reduced thoracic cage compliance in late pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pulmonary conditions**:
  * Pulmonary edema/fluid in alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary fibrosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary hypertension with engorged capillaries [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Thoracic conditions**:
  * Obesity: Increased weight on chest wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Chest wall deformities/rigidity (kyphoscoliosis, ankylosing spondylitis) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Lung volume extremes**: Compliance reduced at very high or very low lung volumes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Multiple factors affect respiratory compliance, with destructive lung diseases typically increasing compliance, while conditions restricting chest wall movement, causing lung stiffness, or altering pleural dynamics generally decrease compliance.

## 13.4. ROLE OF SURFACTANT

**How pulmonary surfactant improves lung compliance.**

### 13.4.1. SURFACE TENSION PROBLEMS

* Surface tension at air-liquid interface in alveoli creates inward force [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Without surfactant, surface tension would cause three major problems:
  * Reduced compliance, requiring high transmural pressure to expand alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Small alveoli would empty into larger ones (per Laplace's law) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fluid would be drawn from interstitium into alveoli (transudation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.4.2. LAPLACE'S LAW

* For a sphere with one surface: P = 2T/r [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * P is transmural pressure required to keep alveolus open [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * T is surface tension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * r is radius of alveolus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Smaller alveoli require higher pressure to remain open at constant surface tension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.4.3. SURFACTANT MECHANISM

* Produced by type II pneumocytes in alveolar walls [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Primarily dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine (DPPC) with surfactant proteins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Hydrophobic end of phospholipid molecule orients toward air, hydrophilic end toward fluid [A:83-84]
* Creates repulsive forces between aligned molecules, opposing water's attractive forces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Surface tension decreases more in smaller alveoli as DPPC molecules are packed closer together [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* This variable effect counters the mechanical disadvantage small alveoli have due to Laplace's law [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.4.4. SURFACTANT FUNCTIONS

* Increases overall lung compliance by reducing surface tension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Stabilizes small alveoli, preventing collapse [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Prevents fluid transudation into alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.4.5. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

* **Infant respiratory distress syndrome (IRDS)**:
  * Occurs in premature neonates with insufficient surfactant [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in poorly compliant lungs with atelectasis and pulmonary edema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Treated with exogenous surfactant or maternal steroids to stimulate production [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Near-drowning**: Surfactant washed away, creating similar clinical picture [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary surfactant reduces surface tension within alveoli, increasing compliance, stabilizing small alveoli against collapse, and preventing fluid transudation, with clinically significant implications in conditions like infant respiratory distress syndrome.

## 13.5. STATIC VS. DYNAMIC COMPLIANCE

**Different methods of measuring compliance and their clinical significance.**

### 13.5.1. STATIC COMPLIANCE

* **Definition**: Compliance measured during no-flow conditions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Measurement technique**:
  * Patient inspires in 500 mL increments [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pause between increments with no gas flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Intrapleural pressure measured using esophageal balloon catheter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Result**: Sigmoid-shaped pressure-volume curve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Low compliance at low lung volumes (flat portion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Maximal compliance near FRC (steepest portion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduced compliance at high volumes (flattening curve) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.5.2. DYNAMIC COMPLIANCE

* **Definition**: Compliance measured during continuous breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Measurement technique**:
  * Continuous pressure and volume measurements throughout respiratory cycle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * No pauses between measurements [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Result**: Dynamic compliance is always lower than static compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical value**: The difference between static and dynamic compliance indirectly measures airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.5.3. TECHNICAL CONSIDERATIONS

* Static compliance more accurately reflects elastic properties [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dynamic compliance affected by both elastic and resistive forces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Respiratory rate affects dynamic compliance (higher rates reduce dynamic compliance) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Airway resistance conditions (e.g., asthma) significantly reduce dynamic compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Static compliance measures only elastic properties (during no-flow conditions), while dynamic compliance includes both elastic and resistive components (during active breathing), with dynamic compliance always lower than static compliance and decreasing further with increased airway resistance.

## 13.6. HYSTERESIS

**Different pressure-volume relationships during inspiration versus expiration.**

### 13.6.1. DEFINITION

* Hysteresis is when measurements differ depending on whether the value is rising or falling [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In lungs, different pressure-volume relationships exist during inspiration compared to expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.6.2. STATIC HYSTERESIS

* Occurs even when airflow ceases between measurements [A:85-86]
* Inspiratory and expiratory static pressure-volume curves follow different paths [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Attributed to viscous resistance of pulmonary surfactant and lung parenchyma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.6.3. DYNAMIC HYSTERESIS

* Creates a pressure-volume loop during continuous breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Loop area represents energy expended as heat in overcoming resistive forces [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Affected by airway resistance and gas flow velocity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Wider loop indicates higher resistance or faster flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Seen in both static and dynamic compliance measurements [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.6.4. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

* Increased dynamic hysteresis in conditions with high airway resistance (e.g., asthma) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Higher respiratory rates widen hysteresis loops by increasing gas flow velocity and turbulence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Hysteresis refers to different pressure-volume relationships during inspiration versus expiration, creating loops whose area represents energy lost as heat, with both static hysteresis (from tissue properties) and dynamic hysteresis (from airflow resistance) contributing to respiratory mechanics.

## 13.7. REGIONAL DIFFERENCES IN COMPLIANCE

**How compliance varies throughout the lung due to gravitational effects.**

### 13.7.1. PLEURAL PRESSURE GRADIENT

* Gravity creates a vertical pleural pressure gradient in upright lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pleural pressure more negative at apex (-10 cmH2O) than at base (-3 cmH2O) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* This gradient affects regional lung inflation and compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.7.2. REGIONAL VARIATIONS AT FRC

* At FRC, apical alveoli are relatively overinflated due to more negative pleural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Basal alveoli are less inflated due to less negative pleural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* The lung apex is "more aerated" than the base at rest [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.7.3. EFFECT ON TIDAL BREATHING

* During inspiration, pleural pressure decreases by ~3 cmH2O throughout the pleural cavity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* This uniform pressure change causes different volume changes in different regions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Basal alveoli expand more than apical alveoli during normal tidal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Basal regions have higher compliance (steeper pressure-volume curve gradient) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Better ventilation occurs in dependent (basal) regions of the lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.7.4. GRAPHICAL REPRESENTATION

* On the static compliance curve, apical regions operate at higher lung volumes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Basal regions operate on the steepest (most compliant) part of the curve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* During inspiration, the volume change is greater in basal regions due to higher compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Regional compliance differences exist throughout the lung due to the gravitational pleural pressure gradient, with dependent (basal) regions showing higher compliance and greater volume change during tidal breathing compared to non-dependent (apical) regions.

## 13.8. OPTIMUM PEEP

**Using compliance curves to determine appropriate ventilator settings.**

### 13.8.1. CLINICAL APPLICATION

* Static compliance curve can guide setting optimal positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Tidal ventilation should occur on the most compliant (steepest) part of pressure-volume curve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.8.2. INFLECTION POINTS

* Lower inflection point: Pressure at which small airways begin to open [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * End-expiration should be above this point to prevent atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * In healthy lungs, typically around 5 cmH2O (similar to physiological PEEP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * In ARDS, may be significantly higher [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Upper inflection point: Pressure at which alveolar overdistension begins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * End-inspiration should be below this point to avoid barotrauma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.8.3. PRACTICAL CHALLENGES

* Measuring static compliance in critically ill patients is difficult [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Requires incremental volume increases with pauses [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * May cause dangerous hypoxemia in ARDS patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dynamic compliance not ideal for determining inflection points [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In practice, PEEP is titrated using clinical judgment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Target adequate tidal volumes (6 mL/kg) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Maintain safe peak and plateau airway pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Balance oxygenation with hemodynamic effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 13.8.4. EVIDENCE FROM CLINICAL TRIALS

* PROBESE trial: Higher PEEP (12 cmH2O) with recruitment maneuvers in obese patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Improved intraoperative oxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Did not reduce postoperative pulmonary complications [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The static compliance curve can theoretically guide optimal PEEP settings by identifying the lower inflection point, but practical challenges often necessitate clinical titration balancing adequate ventilation, oxygenation, and hemodynamic stability.

# 14. WORK OF BREATHING




## 14.1. DEFINITION AND COMPONENTS

**The energy expended to overcome resistive and elastic forces during respiration.**

### 14.1.1. BASIC CONCEPT

* Work of breathing is the energy consumed by respiratory muscles throughout respiratory cycle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Mathematically: Work (J) = Pressure (Pa) × Volume (L) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Technically represents rate of work (power of breathing) measured in watts (J/s) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.1.2. ENERGY REQUIREMENTS

* Normal tidal breathing requires <2% of basal metabolic rate (BMR) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Lung pathology can increase this to up to 30% of BMR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* When excessive, can lead to respiratory muscle fatigue and respiratory failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The work of breathing represents the energy expended by respiratory muscles to overcome resistance during respiration, normally requiring minimal energy but potentially increasing substantially in pathological conditions.

## 14.2. ELASTIC AND RESISTIVE WORK

**The two main components that comprise the work of breathing.**

### 14.2.1. ELASTIC WORK

* **Definition**: Work done to overcome elastic forces during inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Elastic recoil of lung parenchyma (inward force) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Chest wall elasticity (outward force) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Alveolar surface tension (inward force) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Properties**:
  * Represents approximately 65% of normal work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Some energy stored as potential energy rather than dissipated as heat [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Stored energy assists with passive expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Increased by**:
  * Obesity (increased chest wall elastic recoil) [A:88-89]
  * Pulmonary fibrosis (increased lung parenchymal stiffness) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Infant respiratory distress syndrome (increased surface tension) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.2.2. RESISTIVE WORK

* **Definition**: Work done against friction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Energy is "wasted" as heat [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Includes two components:
	* Tissue resistance (10% of resistive work) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Airway resistance (90% of resistive work) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Tissue Resistance**:
  * Friction between tissues during movement [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased in pulmonary fibrosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Airway Resistance**:
  * Friction between gas molecules and airway walls [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Dominant component of resistive work [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The work of breathing includes elastic work (overcoming tissue elasticity and surface tension) and resistive work (overcoming tissue and airway resistance), with elastic work normally predominating and partly recoverable as potential energy.

## 14.3. FACTORS AFFECTING AIRWAY RESISTANCE

**Determinants of resistance to airflow in the respiratory system.**

### 14.3.1. TURBULENCE FACTORS

* **Reynolds Number**: Predicts flow type [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reynolds number = (velocity × diameter × gas density)/gas viscosity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * <2000: 80% likely laminar flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * > 4000: 80% likely turbulent flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 2000-4000: transitional flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Factors promoting turbulent flow**:
  * Higher respiratory rate (increased gas velocity) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased airway diameter (turbulence more common in larger airways) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Upper airway obstruction (increased velocity around obstruction) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased gas density (e.g., deep-sea diving) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical application**: Heliox (helium-oxygen mixture)
  * Lower density reduces Reynolds number [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Promotes laminar flow in narrowed airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Used in upper airway obstruction and diving medicine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.3.2. AIRWAY RADIUS FACTORS

* **Hagen-Poiseuille Law**: Resistance inversely proportional to fourth power of radius [A:89-90]
  * Small reductions in airway radius dramatically increase resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Factors reducing airway radius**:
  * Bronchoconstriction (physiological or pathological) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Low lung volume (reduced radial traction) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Dynamic airway compression during forced expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Particularly significant in COPD due to loss of supportive elastic tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Airway resistance is affected by flow characteristics (laminar vs. turbulent) determined by the Reynolds number, and by airway radius according to the Hagen-Poiseuille law, with small radius reductions causing dramatic resistance increases.

## 14.4. GRAPHICAL REPRESENTATION

**Visualization of the work of breathing on pressure-volume graphs.**

![Work of Breathing Loop](work_of_breathing_loops.png)

### 14.4.1. WORK REPRESENTATION

* Work (pressure × volume) represented as area on pressure-volume graph [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pressure-volume loops show hysteresis (different paths for inspiration and expiration) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Area between inspiratory and expiratory curves represents energy dissipated as heat [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.4.2. NORMAL TIDAL INSPIRATION

* Elastic work appears as triangular area on graph [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Stored as potential energy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Resistive work appears as deviation from ideal diagonal line [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dissipated as heat [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.4.3. NORMAL TIDAL EXPIRATION

* Uses potential energy stored from inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Passive expiration occurs when stored potential energy exceeds resistive work [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Any unused potential energy at end-expiration is dissipated as heat [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The work of breathing can be visualized on pressure-volume graphs, with the area representing work done, and the loop area between inspiratory and expiratory curves specifically representing energy lost as heat due to resistive forces.

## 14.5. CHANGES IN RESTRICTIVE AND OBSTRUCTIVE DISEASE

**How different pathologies affect the work of breathing.**

### 14.5.1. RESTRICTIVE LUNG DISEASE

* **Characteristics**:
  * Reduced lung compliance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Higher intrapleural pressure required for normal tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased elastic work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Relatively normal airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Graphical representation**:
  * Steeper slope (reduced compliance) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Similar width of pressure-volume loop to normal lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Expiration remains passive [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.5.2. OBSTRUCTIVE LUNG DISEASE

* **Characteristics**:
  * Increased airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Higher resistive work during both inspiration and expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * May also have increased compliance (e.g., in COPD) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Graphical representation**:
  * Wider pressure-volume loop (increased resistance) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * If resistance severe enough, expiratory curve bulges to left [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Expiration requires active effort when resistive work exceeds stored potential energy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Active expiratory work appears as separate area on graph [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Restrictive lung diseases primarily increase elastic work (higher pressure needed for the same volume change), while obstructive diseases increase resistive work (wider pressure-volume loop), potentially requiring active expiratory effort if resistance is severe.

## 14.6. ANESTHESIA AND AIRWAY RESISTANCE

**Effects of anesthesia on airway resistance and work of breathing.**

### 14.6.1. FACTORS INCREASING RESISTANCE

* **Low lung volume**:
  * Loss of muscle tone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Supine position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Loss of physiological PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pharyngeal resistance**:
  * Relaxation of pharyngeal muscles causing partial obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Airway devices**:
  * Endotracheal tubes significantly increase resistance per Hagen-Poiseuille law [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Particularly important in pediatric patients (smaller tube radius) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Breathing circuits**:
  * Corrugated tubing, angle-pieces increase turbulence [A:91-92]
  * Heat and moisture exchangers, adjustable pressure-limiting valves add resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Increased gas flow**:
  * Mechanical dead space requires increased minute ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Higher flow rates increase turbulence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Gas properties**:
  * Volatile anesthetics increase gas density and reduce viscosity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Both effects promote turbulence and increase resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 14.6.2. FACTORS DECREASING RESISTANCE

* **Bronchodilation**:
  * Most volatile anesthetics are potent bronchodilators [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increases airway radius, offsetting some resistance effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Halothane particularly effective due to vagolytic effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Extrinsic PEEP**:
  * Increases lung volume, improving radial traction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduces airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Laryngeal mask airways (LMAs)**:
  * Wider bore than ETTs, lower resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Stent pharyngeal tissues open, reducing pharyngeal resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Well-suited for spontaneous ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Anesthesia inevitably increases airway resistance through multiple mechanisms including low lung volume, airway devices, breathing circuits, and gas properties, though some of these effects are offset by bronchodilation from volatile agents and can be minimized with appropriate device selection.

# 15. CONTROL OF VENTILATION




## 15.1. ANATOMICAL SITES

**Neurological structures involved in respiratory control.**

### 15.1.1. COMPONENTS OF RESPIRATORY CONTROL

* **Sensors**:
  * Central and peripheral chemoreceptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary stretch receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * J-receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Irritant receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Joint proprioceptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Control center**:
  * Respiratory nuclei in brainstem [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Effectors**:
  * Respiratory muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Ventilatory control involves a feedback system with multiple sensors monitoring blood gases and mechanical factors, central processing in brainstem respiratory centers, and effector muscles that generate breathing movements.

## 15.2. RESPIRATORY CENTRE

**Brainstem structures that coordinate breathing.**

### 15.2.1. ANATOMICAL AREAS

* **Dorsal respiratory group (DRG)**:
  * Controls diaphragm function [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Responsible for normal tidal inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Ventral respiratory group (VRG)**:
  * Controls intercostal muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Initiates forced expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increases inspiratory force [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Contains pre-Bötzinger complex (respiratory pacemaker) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Apneustic centre**:
  * Modifies DRG activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Prevents lung overexpansion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pneumotaxic centre**:
  * Modifies DRG impulses [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduces depth of inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fine-tunes respiratory pattern [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Can increase respiratory rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The respiratory center consists of four main anatomical regions that coordinate inspiration and expiration, with the DRG and VRG primarily controlling respiratory muscle activity, and the apneustic and pneumotaxic centers modulating respiratory patterns.

## 15.3. INPUTS TO THE RESPIRATORY CENTRE

**Sensors that provide information to the respiratory control system.**

### 15.3.1. PERIPHERAL CHEMORECEPTORS

* **Location**:
  * Carotid bodies at carotid bifurcation (via glossopharyngeal nerve) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Aortic bodies on aortic arch (via vagus nerve) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Carotid bodies more important for respiratory responses [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Characteristics**:
  * High blood flow relative to weight [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Only chemoreceptors that respond to hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Respond to PaO2, not oxygen content (anemia/CO poisoning don't stimulate) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Stimuli**:
  * Low PaO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * High PaCO2 (20% of total CO2 response) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Acidemia (pH < 7.35, carotid bodies only) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hypotension (reduced perfusion increases output) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.3.2. CENTRAL CHEMORECEPTORS

* **Location**:
  * Ventral surface of medulla [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Characteristics**:
  * Respond solely to changes in CSF pH [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Responsible for 80% of response to hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanism**:
  * CO2 diffuses across blood-brain barrier into CSF [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Forms carbonic acid which dissociates to H+ and HCO3- [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * H+ directly stimulates chemoreceptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CSF more sensitive to PCO2 changes due to lower protein concentration/buffering [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Response characteristics**:
  * Respiratory acidosis more stimulating than metabolic acidosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Profound metabolic acidosis still stimulates via carotid bodies [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cerebral vasodilation during hypercapnea enhances central chemoreceptor mechanism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Chronic adaptation**:
  * In chronic hypercapnea (e.g., some COPD patients), ventilatory drive diminishes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * HCO3actively secreted into CSF normalizes pH [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Central chemoreceptors no longer stimulated [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Kidneys reabsorb more HCO3normalizing arterial pH [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in CO2 retention with reliance on hypoxemic drive alone [A:94-95]
  * ⚠️ Caution when administering O2 to such patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.3.3. MECHANORECEPTORS

* **Lung stretch receptors**:
  * Located in bronchial smooth muscle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Respond to lung overinflation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Signal via vagus nerve to apneustic center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduce depth of inspiration (Hering-Breuer reflex) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Limited role in normal adult tidal breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Muscle spindles**:
  * May initiate ventilatory response to exercise [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.3.4. OTHER INPUTS

* **Juxtacapillary receptors (J-receptors)**:
  * Non-myelinated C-fibers in alveolar walls [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * When activated cause increased ventilation, dyspnea, bradycardia, hypotension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Stimulated by pulmonary edema, pulmonary emboli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Irritant receptors**:
  * Located in airway epithelium [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Stimulate ventilation in response to noxious gases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Cause bronchoconstriction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pain receptors**:
  * Activate respiratory center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Temperature receptors**:
  * Increased core temperature stimulates ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Cortical inputs**:
  * Allow voluntary control [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Can temporarily override chemical control [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * "Break point" occurs when chemical drive eventually overcomes voluntary control [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Limbic system**:
  * Emotional states stimulate respiratory center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Can cause hyperventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The respiratory center receives inputs from multiple sources, with chemical control via central and peripheral chemoreceptors being primary, but mechanical, pain, temperature, and cortical influences also playing important roles in modulating ventilation.

## 15.4. VENTILATORY RESPONSE TO HYPOXEMIA

**How the respiratory system responds to low oxygen levels.**

### 15.4.1. RESPONSE CHARACTERISTICS

* Ventilation increases significantly when PaO2 falls below 8 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Detected by peripheral chemoreceptors (primarily carotid bodies) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Response occurs rapidly (within seconds) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Augmented in the presence of hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.4.2. RESPONSE CURVE

* Minimal ventilatory response until PaO2 < 8 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dramatic increase in ventilation as PaO2 falls further [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Maximum response occurs on steepest part of oxygen dissociation curve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Prolonged severe hypoxemia eventually depresses respiratory center, causing apnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.4.3. ANESTHETIC EFFECTS

* Anesthetic agents significantly depress hypoxic ventilatory response [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ Increased risk of respiratory depression with mild hypoxemia during recovery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The ventilatory response to hypoxemia is mediated by peripheral chemoreceptors, becomes significant when PaO2 falls below 8 kPa, is enhanced by concurrent hypercapnea, and can be substantially depressed by anesthetic agents.

## 15.5. VENTILATORY RESPONSE TO HYPERCAPNEA

**How the respiratory system responds to elevated carbon dioxide levels.**

### 15.5.1. RESPONSE CHARACTERISTICS

* Linear response between PaCO2 5-10 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Ventilation increases by 2-3 L/min per mmHg increase in PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Central chemoreceptors provide 80% of response [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Peripheral chemoreceptors provide 20% of response (but act faster) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.5.2. RESPONSE CURVE

* Ventilation increases linearly with PaCO2 within physiological range [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Experiments in animals show ventilation may decrease at profound hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Right-shifted by anesthetic agents and opioids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The ventilatory response to hypercapnea shows a linear relationship between PaCO2 and minute ventilation in the physiological range, with central chemoreceptors providing the majority of the response but peripheral chemoreceptors responding more rapidly.

## 15.6. DRUG EFFECTS ON VENTILATION

**How anesthetic and analgesic medications affect respiratory control.**

### 15.6.1. VOLATILE ANESTHETIC AGENTS

* Dose-dependent depression of ventilatory control [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Blunt response to hypercapnea to varying degrees [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Relative potencies: enflurane > desflurane > isoflurane > sevoflurane > halothane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Exceptions: N2O (no effect), ether (may increase ventilation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.2. INTRAVENOUS ANESTHETIC AGENTS

* Initial respiratory stimulation followed by depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Propofol: Depresses laryngeal reflexes (advantageous for LMA insertion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Propofol: May abolish peripheral chemoreceptor response to hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Ketamine: Generally preserves airway reflexes and spontaneous ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.3. OPIOIDS

* Significant depression of respiratory center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Right-shift of PaCO2-ventilation response curve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Higher resting PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Antitussive effect (cough reflex depression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Rare chest wall rigidity with rapid IV administration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.4. BENZODIAZEPINES

* Respiratory center depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Decreased respiratory rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Blunted response to hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.5. MUSCLE RELAXANTS

* Respiratory muscle paralysis requiring mechanical ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Specific effects:
  * Atracurium: May cause histamine release and bronchospasm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Suxamethonium/mivacurium: Prolonged action with reduced plasma cholinesterase [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.6. NSAIDS

* May precipitate bronchospasm in susceptible asthmatics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 15.6.7. NEURAXIAL LOCAL ANESTHETICS

* High spinal anesthesia may cause intercostal muscle weakness [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Diaphragm usually unaffected (innervated by C3-5) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Most anesthetic medications depress ventilatory control to varying degrees, with volatile and intravenous anesthetics, opioids, and benzodiazepines all affecting central respiratory drive, while muscle relaxants directly affect the effector muscles without influencing central control.

# 16. PULMONARY CIRCULATION




## 16.1. UNIQUE FEATURES

**Distinctive characteristics of the pulmonary circulation compared to the systemic circulation.**

### 16.1.1. GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS

* Low-pressure, low-resistance, high-flow circulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* 100% of cardiac output (5 L/min) flows through with mean pulmonary artery pressure (MPAP) of only 15 mmHg [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.1.2. ANATOMICAL FEATURES

* Two pulmonary arteries (one per lung) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* At least four pulmonary veins (two from each lung) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Arteries, veins, and bronchi initially run together, dividing at same points [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In lung periphery:
  * Veins pass between lung lobules [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Arteries and bronchi travel together to lobule centers [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pulmonary capillaries are fragile and narrow (7-10 μm) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Just wide enough for red blood cells to squeeze through [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.1.3. BRONCHIAL CIRCULATION

* Separate blood supply to conducting airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Receives 3-5% of cardiac output [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Some deoxygenated blood drains via pulmonary veins, creating anatomical shunt [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.1.4. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

**Lung Transplantation**:
* Historically, bronchial arteries were not re-anastomosed during lung transplant [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Most grafts survived despite loss of bronchial arterial supply [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* However, some patients develop ischemic bronchiolitis obliterans [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Recent trend toward direct bronchial artery revascularization [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The pulmonary circulation is a low-pressure, high-flow system with unique anatomical features including fragile capillaries and a parallel bronchial circulation, requiring relatively little pressure to propel the entire cardiac output through the lungs.

## 16.2. LOW PRESSURE SYSTEM

**Why the pulmonary circulation can operate at much lower pressures than the systemic circulation.**

![Pulmonary vs Systemic Pressures](pulmonary_vs_systemic_pressures.png)

### 16.2.1. FUNCTIONAL REQUIREMENTS

* Unlike systemic circulation, pulmonary circulation doesn't need to direct blood flow between regions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Exception: Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction redirects blood from hypoxic areas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Only needs pressure sufficient to propel blood to lung apex [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.2.2. CAPILLARY STRUCTURE

* Pulmonary capillaries nearly surrounded by alveolar gas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Very fragile with minimal connective tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Prone to distension or collapse [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.2.3. TRANSUDATION PREVENTION

* Low transudation of fluid from capillary to alveolus depends on low transmural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Transmural pressure = difference between capillary and alveolar pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Low pulmonary capillary pressure essential to prevent pulmonary edema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The pulmonary circulation maintains low pressure to prevent pulmonary edema and capillary damage, which is possible because it doesn't need to overcome gravity to the same degree as the systemic circulation or direct flow to specific regions.

## 16.3. VASCULAR RESISTANCES COMPARISON

**Mathematical comparison between pulmonary and systemic vascular resistances.**

### 16.3.1. DARCY'S LAW

* Similar to Ohm's law for electrical circuits [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pressure difference = total flow × vascular resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Rearranged: vascular resistance = pressure difference/total flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.3.2. RESISTANCE CALCULATION FORMULAS

![PVR](PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PULMONARY_SYSTEM_PVR.001.jpeg_eb1441.jpeg_b0a6_.jpeg)

* **Pulmonary Vascular Resistance (PVR)**:
  * PVR = 80 × (MPAP - PCWP)/CO [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * MPAP = mean pulmonary artery pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PCWP = pulmonary capillary wedge pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * CO = cardiac output [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * 80 = conversion factor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR)**:
  * SVR = 80 × (MAP - mean right atrial pressure)/CO [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * MAP = mean arterial pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.3.3. TYPICAL VALUES

* **PVR calculation**:
  * MPAP = 15 mmHg, PCWP = 5 mmHg, CO = 5 L/min [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PVR = 80 × (15-5)/5 = 160 dyn·s·cm-5 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **SVR calculation**:
  * MAP = 100 mmHg, mean right atrial pressure = 2 mmHg, CO = 5 L/min [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * SVR = 80 × (100-2)/5 = 1568 dyn·s·cm-5 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Comparison**:
  * PVR is approximately one-tenth of SVR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Measurements obtainable using pulmonary artery catheter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary vascular resistance is approximately one-tenth of systemic vascular resistance, reflecting the low-pressure, low-resistance nature of the pulmonary circulation compared to the systemic circulation.

## 16.4. FACTORS AFFECTING PVR

**Physiological and pathological influences on pulmonary vascular resistance.**

### 16.4.1. PULMONARY ARTERY PRESSURE

* Increased MPAP significantly reduces PVR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Two mechanisms:
  * **Recruitment**: Opening of collapsed pulmonary capillaries (primary at low MPAP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Distension**: Further widening of open pulmonary capillaries (primary at high MPAP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* During exercise, despite large CO increase, MPAP rises only moderately due to recruitment/distension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* In racehorses, recruitment/distension capacity can be exceeded, causing pulmonary hemorrhage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.4.2. LUNG VOLUMES

* Two functional vessel types:
  * **Alveolar capillaries**: Compressed by raised alveolar pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * **Extra-alveolar vessels**: Pulled open by radial traction from surrounding lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PVR is minimal at FRC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased at high lung volumes:
  * Alveolar capillaries become stretched and distorted [A:98-99]
  * Resistance to flow increases significantly [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Extra-alveolar vessels contribute little resistance (radial traction increases diameter) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Increased at low lung volumes:
  * Extra-alveolar vessels narrow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * High resistance to flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.4.3. HYPOXIC PULMONARY VASOCONSTRICTION (HPV)

* **Definition**: Vasoconstriction of pulmonary arterioles in response to alveolar hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanism**:
  * Main stimulus is low alveolar PO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Mixed venous PO2 contributes ~20% of response [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Continues in denervated lungs (not neurally mediated) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Likely mediators:
	* Nitric oxide (NO): Produced in endothelium when PAO2 > 9.3 kPa; decreased in hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* O2-sensitive K+ channels: Stimulated by hypoxia, leading to membrane depolarization and Ca2+ influx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Response characteristics**:
  * Primarily affects pulmonary arterioles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Some venous contribution (pulmonary venules) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Rapid onset (seconds) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary lobar blood flow can halve within minutes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Prolonged hypoxia causes second phase of sustained vasoconstriction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.4.4. OTHER FACTORS AFFECTING PVR

* **Increasing PVR**:
  * Hypercarbia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Acidosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Catecholamines (adrenaline, noradrenaline) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Vasoactive mediators (thromboxane A2, angiotensin II, serotonin, histamine) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Extremes of lung volume (high or low) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Decreasing PVR**:
  * Volatile anesthetics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hypocarbia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Alkalosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hyperoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Vasodilators (isoprenaline, acetylcholine, prostacyclin, NO) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * High intrathoracic pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased pulmonary venous pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** PVR is affected by multiple factors including pressure (recruitment/distension), lung volume (showing a U-shaped relationship with minimum PVR at FRC), and hypoxia (causing vasoconstriction), along with numerous chemical and pharmacological influences.

## 16.5. HYPOXIC PULMONARY VASOCONSTRICTION

![Hypoxic Pulmonary Vasoconstriction](hypoxic_pulmonary_vasoconstriction.png)

**The unique response of pulmonary vessels to low oxygen tension.**

### 16.5.1. CONTRAST WITH SYSTEMIC CIRCULATION

* **Systemic vessels**: Vasodilate in response to tissue hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pulmonary vessels**: Vasoconstrict in response to alveolar hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* This opposite response is physiologically beneficial in both circulations [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.5.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL PURPOSE

* Redirects blood from poorly ventilated to better ventilated areas [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reduces V/Q mismatch by matching perfusion to ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Improves overall gas exchange efficiency [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.5.3. THRESHOLD AND MAGNITUDE

* Significant vasoconstriction when PAO2 < 8 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Can reduce regional blood flow by up to 50% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
