**Bottom Line:** Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction is a unique physiological response that diverts blood away from poorly ventilated lung regions, helping to maintain V/Q matching and optimize gas exchange.

## 16.6. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS OF HPV

**Physiological and pathological situations where hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction plays a key role.**

### 16.6.1. FETAL CIRCULATION

* In utero, fetal lungs unexpanded with PAO2 of zero [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Widespread HPV causes high PVR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Only 10% of fetal cardiac output passes through lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Blood redirected through ductus arteriosus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* At first breath, oxygen enters alveoli, PVR decreases dramatically, increasing pulmonary blood flow [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.2. INFANT RESPIRATORY DISTRESS SYNDROME

* Lack of surfactant increases surface tension [A:99-100]
* Results in widespread atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* First breath extremely difficult [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PVR remains high due to HPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Poor alveolar perfusion leads to pneumocyte death [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Necrotic pneumocytes form hyaline membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Barrier impairs diffusion, increases HPV, creating vicious cycle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.3. PNEUMONIA

* HPV increases local PVR near hypoxic alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Redirects blood to better-ventilated alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Reduces V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.4. HIGH ALTITUDE

* Low barometric pressure causes generalized alveolar hypoxia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in generalized pulmonary vasoconstriction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Includes pulmonary venoconstriction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Implicated in high-altitude pulmonary edema development [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.5. CARDIOGENIC PULMONARY EDEMA

* In left ventricular failure, pulmonary venous pressure increases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Greater pressure in lung bases due to West zones [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Fluid transudation across alveolar-capillary barrier (pulmonary edema) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Basal alveoli become hypoxic [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* HPV diverts blood to better-ventilated apices ("upper lobe diversion") [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.6. CLINICAL MODIFICATION OF HPV

* **Inhalational anesthetics**:
  * Inhibit HPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Can worsen shunt in pneumonia patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Significant effect only above 1 MAC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **One-lung ventilation**:
  * HPV reduces blood flow to unventilated lung [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Minimizes V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Nitrous oxide**: Minimal effect on HPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Intravenous agents**: No direct effect on HPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.6.7. CHRONIC HYPOXIC PULMONARY HYPERTENSION

* Some patients with chronic lung disease develop pulmonary arterial hypertension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Thought to be due to widespread HPV [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Can lead to right ventricular failure (cor pulmonale) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction plays critical roles in fetal circulatory transition, various pulmonary pathologies, high-altitude adaptation, and clinical scenarios like one-lung ventilation, with its inhibition by inhalational anesthetics having important implications for ventilation-perfusion matching.

## 16.7. PULMONARY ARTERY CATHETER

**Clinical uses and waveform characteristics of flow-directed pulmonary artery catheters.**

### 16.7.1. APPLICATIONS

* **Diagnostic uses**:
  * Pulmonary hypertension [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Acute respiratory distress syndrome [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Measurement capabilities**:
  * Hemodynamic parameters (e.g., cardiac output) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Mixed venous oxygen saturation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Therapeutic uses**:
  * Air emboli aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.7.2. CLINICAL CONSIDERATIONS

* Usage has declined in recent years [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* No mortality reduction demonstrated in general ICU population [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Associated complications:
  * Line infection [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary infarction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary artery rupture [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 16.7.3. CATHETER INSERTION PROCESS

* **Insertion procedure**:
  * 150 cm catheter inserted through right internal jugular vein sheath [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pressure at catheter tip transduced and used for location identification [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pressure waveform progression**:
  * Right atrium: low-pressure waveform with a and v waves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Right ventricle: systolic/diastolic pattern with high systolic, low diastolic pressures [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary artery: systolic/diastolic pattern with dicrotic notch, higher diastolic pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Wedge position: attenuated waveform reflecting left atrial pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Catheter advancement**:
  * Advanced ~20 cm to right ventricle [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Balloon inflated to guide catheter through pulmonary valve [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Flows with blood until wedging in branch of pulmonary artery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Measurement of pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (PCWP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Flow-directed pulmonary artery catheters allow measurement of right heart pressures, cardiac output, and mixed venous oxygen saturation, with distinctive pressure waveforms marking passage through each cardiac chamber, though their routine use has declined due to lack of demonstrated mortality benefit and potential complications.

# 17. OXYGEN TOXICITY




## 17.1. DEFINITION AND AFFECTED SYSTEMS

**The harmful effects of excessive oxygen exposure on various body systems.**

### 17.1.1. BASIC CONCEPT

* Breathing oxygen at high partial pressures can damage tissues [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Multiple organ systems can be affected [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.1.2. PRIMARILY AFFECTED SYSTEMS

* **Central nervous system (CNS)**:
  * Causes seizures and unconsciousness [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Known as Paul Bert effect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Particularly important for divers breathing hyperbaric oxygen [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Lungs**:
  * Causes bronchopulmonary dysplasia in premature neonates [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * In older children and adults: absorption atelectasis, ARDS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Known as Lorraine Smith effect [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Retina**:
  * Leads to retrolental fibroplasia in neonates [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Also known as retinopathy of prematurity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Oxygen toxicity can affect multiple organ systems, with the CNS, lungs, and retina being most vulnerable, manifesting as different syndromes depending on the affected system.

## 17.2. MECHANISM OF TOXICITY

**Biochemical basis for oxygen's paradoxical toxicity.**

### 17.2.1. OXYGEN MOLECULE STRUCTURE

* Oxygen molecule contains two oxygen atoms [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Each atom has an unpaired electron in outer shell [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* These unpaired electrons give O2 its paramagnetic property [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Molecules with unpaired electrons (free radicals) are typically highly reactive [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.2.2. CONTROLLED REACTIVITY

* Oxygen molecule's unpaired electrons are relatively unreactive [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Requires metal ions (e.g., in metalloproteins) to accept electrons [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Important for controlled electron transport chain function [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Cytochrome c oxidase (Complex IV) contains copper, facilitates O2 reduction to water [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.2.3. REACTIVE OXYGEN SPECIES

* During O2 reduction to water, intermediate oxidation states form [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* These include:
  * Superoxide anion (O2•-) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hydroxyl free radicals (OH•) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Collectively known as reactive oxygen species (ROS) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Also generated by ionizing radiation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.2.4. CELLULAR DAMAGE

* ROS cause harmful effects on:
  * Nucleic acids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Fatty acids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Amino acids [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Sulphydryl-containing enzymes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Oxygen toxicity results from the formation of reactive oxygen species during oxygen metabolism, which damage cellular components including nucleic acids, lipids, proteins, and enzymes.

## 17.3. NATURAL ANTIOXIDANT SYSTEMS

**Endogenous mechanisms that protect against oxidative stress.**

### 17.3.1. ANTIOXIDANT TYPES

* **Exogenous**: Obtained from diet (e.g., ascorbic acid/vitamin C) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Endogenous**: Produced by the body [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.3.2. KEY ENDOGENOUS SYSTEMS

* **Glutathione system** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Catalase system** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Superoxide dismutase system** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.3.3. LIMITATIONS

* During oxidative stress, antioxidant systems can be overwhelmed by ROS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in cell damage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The body has several endogenous antioxidant systems that normally protect against oxidative damage, but these can be overwhelmed during periods of excessive oxygen exposure or oxidative stress.

## 17.4. ROS PHYSIOLOGICAL ROLES

**The beneficial functions of reactive oxygen species in normal physiology.**

### 17.4.1. IMMUNE SYSTEM FUNCTIONS

* Neutrophils and macrophages synthesize and secrete ROS [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ROS kill phagocytosed bacteria [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Process called respiratory burst (increased O2 consumption) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.4.2. THYROID FUNCTION

* Thyroid follicular cells synthesize H2O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* H2O2 oxidizes iodine anions (I-) to iodine (I2) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Essential step in thyroid hormone synthesis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Despite their potential toxicity, reactive oxygen species serve essential physiological functions, including microbial killing by immune cells and thyroid hormone synthesis.

## 17.5. OXYGEN EXPOSURE LIMITS

**Safe thresholds for oxygen exposure and factors affecting toxicity risk.**

### 17.5.1. SAFE THRESHOLD

* Inspired O2 below 50 kPa generally considered safe [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Equivalent to FiO2 of 50% at atmospheric pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.5.2. RISK FACTORS

* Risk increases with:
  * Higher inspired partial pressure of O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Longer duration of exposure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.5.3. SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR DIVERS

* More susceptible to CNS toxicity due to hyperbaric breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Loss of consciousness underwater particularly dangerous [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Calculate maximum exposure times based on:
  * Depth of dive [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Total time underwater [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Oxygen toxicity risk depends on both the partial pressure of oxygen and exposure duration, with 50% oxygen at atmospheric pressure generally considered safe, though special considerations apply to divers breathing hyperbaric oxygen.

## 17.6. CLINICAL CONSIDERATIONS

**Application of oxygen toxicity concepts in clinical settings.**

### 17.6.1. INTENSIVE CARE OXYGENATION

* Evidence of reduced pulmonary function in healthy volunteers after 24h of 100% O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* ARDS features may develop after 48h of 100% O2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Significant variability between patients in susceptibility [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Most ICU guidelines recommend:
  * Keeping FiO2 below 50% when possible [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Minimizing exposure time to higher FiO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Never withholding necessary oxygen from hypoxemic patients due to toxicity concerns [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Clinical oxygen administration balances the risk of oxygen toxicity against the need to treat hypoxemia, with guidelines generally recommending limiting FiO2 to below 50% when possible while never withholding oxygen from patients who need it.

## 17.7. BLEOMYCIN AND OXYGEN TOXICITY

**The special relationship between the chemotherapy agent bleomycin and oxygen toxicity.**

### 17.7.1. BLEOMYCIN BASICS

* Chemotherapy drug used for Hodgkin's lymphoma and testicular carcinoma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Most serious side effect is pulmonary fibrosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Typically occurs within first 6 months of treatment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.7.2. BLEOMYCIN-OXYGEN INTERACTION

* Patients who have received bleomycin and subsequently require supplemental oxygen are at increased risk [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Rapidly developing life-threatening pulmonary fibrosis can occur [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Risk factors:
  * Prior bleomycin treatment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Subsequent oxygen administration (e.g., for pneumonia or general anesthesia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.7.3. CLINICAL MANAGEMENT

* Patients carry alert cards [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Oxygen administration should be avoided when possible [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* If absolutely necessary, oxygen should be titrated to SaO2 of 88-92% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 17.7.4. MECHANISM

* The exact mechanism remains controversial [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Bleomycin chelates iron [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Bleomycin-iron complex reacts with oxygen [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Produces ROS (superoxide and hydroxide) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* These ROS cleave DNA [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Additional oxygen may increase ROS production beyond body's antioxidant capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Patients who have received bleomycin chemotherapy are at increased risk for oxygen-induced lung injury, necessitating cautious oxygen administration with target saturations of 88-92% if supplemental oxygen is absolutely required.

# 18. VENTILATORY FAILURE




## 18.1. DEFINITION AND TYPES OF RESPIRATORY FAILURE

**Classification and diagnostic criteria for respiratory failure.**

### 18.1.1. BASIC DEFINITION

* Respiratory failure occurs when the respiratory system fails in one or both main functions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]:
  * Oxygenation of blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Carbon dioxide elimination [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.1.2. CLASSIFICATION

* **Type 1 respiratory failure**:
  * Definition: PaO2 < 8.0 kPa with normal or low PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Represents oxygenation failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Type 2 respiratory failure**:
  * Definition: PaO2 < 8.0 kPa with raised PaCO2 > 6.0 kPa [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Represents ventilatory failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Respiratory failure is classified into type 1 (oxygenation failure with normal or low CO2) and type 2 (ventilatory failure with hypoxemia and hypercapnia), reflecting different underlying pathophysiological processes.

## 18.2. OXYGENATION VS. VENTILATION

**The distinction between the gas exchange and mechanical functions of the respiratory system.**

### 18.2.1. RESPIRATORY SYSTEM COMPONENTS

* **Gas-exchange system**:
  * Alveolar-capillary units [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pulmonary circulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Responsible for oxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Deficiency leads to hypoxemia and type 1 respiratory failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Bellows system**:
  * Chest wall and pleura [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Respiratory muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Airways [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Nerves [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Respiratory center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Responsible for ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Moves air from atmosphere to alveoli and back [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Facilitates CO2 diffusion from pulmonary capillaries to alveoli [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.2.2. RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN VENTILATION AND CO2

* Alveolar ventilation (VA) inversely proportional to PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Distinct from minute ventilation (VE = respiratory rate × tidal volume) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** The respiratory system can be conceptualized as having two functional components: a gas-exchange system responsible for oxygenation, and a bellows system responsible for ventilation and CO2 elimination, with failure of either component leading to different types of respiratory failure.

## 18.3. PATHOPHYSIOLOGICAL PROCESSES

**Mechanisms leading to type 2 respiratory failure.**

### 18.3.1. VENTILATORY CONTROL MECHANISM

* Normal ventilation controlled by negative feedback [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]:
  * Rising PaCO2 stimulates respiratory center via chemoreceptors [A:104-105]
  * Respiratory center signals respiratory muscles [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased VE and VA reduce PaCO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* This system maintains PaCO2 within tight limits [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PaCO2 > 6.0 kPa indicates inadequate alveolar ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.3.2. FAILURE MECHANISMS

* **Respiratory center failure**:
  * Depression by opioids or general anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reflex desensitization to high PaCO2 to prevent muscle fatigue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Chest wall movement problems**:
  * Mechanical issues (e.g., flail chest) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Neuropathic conditions (e.g., Guillain-Barré syndrome) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Myopathies [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Respiratory muscle fatigue**:
  * Inability to synthesize sufficient ATP despite intact drive and chest wall [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Occurs when energy demand exceeds supply [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Type 2 respiratory failure occurs when the respiratory center fails to respond appropriately to rising CO2, when the chest wall cannot move effectively, or when respiratory muscles fatigue despite adequate central drive, all resulting in inadequate alveolar ventilation.

## 18.4. MYASTHENIA GRAVIS AND RESPIRATORY FAILURE

**How myasthenia gravis can lead to respiratory failure.**

### 18.4.1. PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

* Autoimmune disease targeting nicotinic acetylcholine receptors at neuromuscular junction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Antagonism and destruction of receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Results in weakness of voluntary muscles with characteristic fatigability [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Common muscle groups affected: ocular, facial, bulbar, limb [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Respiratory muscles usually only mildly affected [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.4.2. RESPIRATORY COMPLICATIONS

* During myasthenic crisis, respiratory muscles can become profoundly weak [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Inspiratory muscles too weak to maintain normal tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Breathing becomes shallow and rapid [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.4.3. MECHANISMS OF RESPIRATORY FAILURE

* **Ineffective ventilation**:
  * Dead space makes up higher proportion of shallow tidal volumes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Alveolar ventilation decreases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in hypercapnea and type 2 respiratory failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Worsening mechanics**:
  * Small inspiratory volumes cause microatelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Lung compliance decreases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Work of breathing increases further [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Secondary complications**:
  * Pneumonia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Aspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Poor cough and secretion clearance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Creates V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** In myasthenia gravis, respiratory failure develops when respiratory muscle weakness leads to shallow breathing patterns, where dead space ventilation predominates over alveolar ventilation, with progressive mechanical disadvantage from microatelectasis and potential complications from poor secretion clearance.

## 18.5. COPD AND CHRONIC HYPERCAPNEA

**Mechanisms and adaptations in chronic CO2 retention.**

### 18.5.1. COPD CHARACTERISTICS

* Small airways obstruction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Destruction of lung elastic tissue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Chest hyperinflation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.5.2. MECHANICAL DISADVANTAGES

* **Diaphragmatic flattening**:
  * Puts diaphragm at mechanical disadvantage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Requires greater force to generate same negative intrapleural pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Increased thoracic diameter**:
  * Increases intercostal muscle fiber length [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Suboptimal sarcomere overlap [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * More energy required for same chest wall movement [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.5.3. CHRONIC HYPERCAPNEA DEVELOPMENT

* Subset of COPD patients develops chronic CO2 retention [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Mechanism not fully understood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Key observations:
  * Patients can voluntarily bring PaCO2 to normal levels temporarily [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Develop respiratory muscle fatigue after few minutes of hyperventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Suggests respiratory center "chooses" to hypoventilate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Prevents respiratory muscle fatigue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Process known as reflex desensitization of respiratory center [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Represents optimization over homeostasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** In some COPD patients, chronic hypercapnea develops not from an inability to eliminate CO2, but from a protective adaptation where the respiratory center "accepts" higher CO2 levels to prevent respiratory muscle fatigue that would occur if normal CO2 levels were maintained.

## 18.6. COPD EXACERBATIONS

**Acute worsening of COPD and its impact on ventilation.**

### 18.6.1. DEFINITION

* Acute change in dyspnea, cough, and/or sputum beyond normal day-to-day variations [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Often infective in origin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Other causes include atmospheric pollution [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.6.2. PATHOPHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES

* Significant increase in resistive and elastic work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Dynamic airway obstruction during expiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Development of intrinsic PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Additional work required to overcome intrinsic PEEP during next inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.6.3. RESPIRATORY FAILURE PROGRESSION

* COPD patients (especially with chronic hypercapnea) susceptible to respiratory muscle fatigue [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* High inspiratory work during exacerbations [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Metabolic demand exceeds supply [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Alveolar ventilation decreases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* PaCO2 rises [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 18.6.4. MANAGEMENT APPROACH

* Respiratory support often required [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Invasive or non-invasive ventilation options [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Non-invasive ventilation mechanisms:
  * Extrinsic PEEP offsets intrinsic PEEP and dynamic hyperinflation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Pressure support overcomes increased inspiratory airway resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Restores tidal volume to normal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Evidence for non-invasive ventilation:
  * Reduces need for invasive ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduces ICU complications [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Shortens ICU and hospital stays [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** During COPD exacerbations, increased work of breathing, intrinsic PEEP, and airway resistance overwhelm respiratory muscles, leading to decreased alveolar ventilation and rising CO2. Non-invasive ventilation can effectively offset these changes by providing extrinsic PEEP and pressure support.

# 19. ANESTHESIA AND THE LUNG




## 19.1. EFFECTS OF ANESTHETIC DRUGS

**How anesthetic medications impact respiratory function.**

### 19.1.1. VOLATILE ANESTHETIC AGENTS

* **Respiratory pattern effects**:
  * Increase respiratory rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduce tidal volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Ventilatory control effects**:
  * Blunt response to hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Varying potencies: enflurane > desflurane > isoflurane > sevoflurane > halothane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Exceptions: N2O (no effect), ether (may increase ventilation) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.1.2. INTRAVENOUS ANESTHETIC AGENTS

* **General effects**:
  * Initial respiratory stimulation after induction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Followed by respiratory center depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Tidal volume falls [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Apnea may occur, especially with propofol [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Agent-specific effects**:
  * Propofol: Depresses laryngeal reflexes (advantageous for LMA insertion) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Propofol: May abolish peripheral chemoreceptor response to hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Ketamine: Preserves airway reflexes and spontaneous ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.1.3. OTHER ANESTHETIC MEDICATIONS

* **Opioids**:
  * Significant respiratory center depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Reduced respiratory rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Blunted response to hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Antitussive action (cough reflex depression) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Histamine release (with morphine) potentially causing bronchospasm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Rare chest wall rigidity with rapid IV administration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Benzodiazepines**:
  * Respiratory center depression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Decreased respiratory rate [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Blunted response to hypercapnea [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Muscle relaxants**:
  * Respiratory muscle paralysis necessitating mechanical ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Atracurium: May cause histamine release and bronchospasm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Suxamethonium/mivacurium: Prolonged action with reduced plasma cholinesterase [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **NSAIDs**: May precipitate bronchospasm in susceptible asthmatics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Neuraxial local anesthetics**:
  * High spinal anesthesia can cause intercostal muscle weakness [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Diaphragm usually unaffected (innervated by C3-5) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Most anesthetic drugs have significant effects on respiratory function, with volatile and intravenous agents affecting respiratory patterns and depressing ventilatory control, opioids and benzodiazepines depressing respiratory center drive, and muscle relaxants directly affecting respiratory muscle function.

## 19.2. EFFECTS OF GENERAL ANESTHESIA

**Physiological changes in the respiratory system during anesthesia.**

### 19.2.1. AIRWAY EFFECTS

* **Laryngospasm and bronchospasm**:
  * Airway manipulation can trigger reflexive spasm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Bronchospasm more common in patients with reactive airways (asthmatics, smokers) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Laryngospasm more common in children, particularly with inhalational inductions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Propofol's laryngeal reflex depression advantageous for LMA insertion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Humidification**:
  * ETTs and LMAs bypass upper airway [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Loss of normal warming, humidification, and filtering functions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Typically replaced by heat and moisture exchanger [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Inadequate humidification leads to thick, tenacious secretions and mucous plugging [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Loss of physiological PEEP**:
  * ETTs bypass larynx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Vocal cords normally maintain 3-4 cmH2O of PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Loss predisposes to atelectasis and V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Standard practice to apply 3-5 cmH2O extrinsic PEEP after intubation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * PEEP increases intrathoracic pressure, potentially reducing venous return [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * May be disadvantageous with raised ICP or during tonsillectomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Increased work of breathing**:
  * Airway devices increase resistance to gas flow based on internal radius [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * LMA's larger radius makes it suitable for spontaneous ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * ETT's narrower radius significantly increases work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Particularly important in small children and when weaning ICU patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased dead space requires higher minute ventilation to maintain alveolar ventilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.2.2. LUNG VOLUME CHANGES

* **FRC reduction** due to:
  * Supine position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Chest wall muscle relaxation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Co-morbidities (acute abdomen, obesity) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Patient population (pediatric, obstetric) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Closing capacity implications**:
  * FRC falls below closing capacity in middle-aged patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Optimization strategies**:
  * Semi-recumbent position maximizes FRC during induction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Obese patients particularly prone to hypoxemia despite preoxygenation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Intraoperative positioning (lithotomy, Trendelenburg) further reduces FRC [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Secondary effects of low lung volume**:
  * Reduced airway radius increases resistance [A:108-109]
  * Increased pulmonary vascular resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.2.3. ATELECTASIS MECHANISMS

* **Absorption atelectasis**:
  * Breathing room air: N2 "splints" alveoli open [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * During anesthesia: Diffusible gases (O2, N2O) and small airway closure lead to complete gas absorption [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in alveolar collapse [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Compression atelectasis**:
  * Reduced diaphragmatic tone [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Abdominal content compression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Causes basal atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Exacerbated by:
	* Loss of physiological PEEP [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
	* Increased abdominal pressure (pneumoperitoneum) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.2.4. V/Q MISMATCH

* Results from:
  * Low lung volume causing small airway closure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increased atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Positive pressure ventilation altering intrathoracic pressure and pulmonary capillary dynamics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * West zone 1 conditions at lung apex [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction impairment by volatile anesthetics [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Management**:
  * Hypoxemia typically managed with increased FiO2 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Addition of extrinsic PEEP to reduce atelectasis and increase lung volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** General anesthesia affects the respiratory system through multiple mechanisms including airway device effects, lung volume reduction, atelectasis development through absorption and compression, and V/Q mismatch, all potentially contributing to hypoxemia unless appropriately managed.

## 19.3. POST-OPERATIVE RESPIRATORY FUNCTION

**Respiratory changes and complications in the post-operative period.**

### 19.3.1. COMMON PULMONARY COMPLICATIONS

* Atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Bronchospasm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pneumonia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Pulmonary edema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Ventilatory failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.3.2. RISK FACTORS

* **Patient-related factors**:
  * Underlying lung and cardiac disease [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Procedure-related factors**:
  * Upper abdominal and thoracic surgery carry highest risk [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Associated with reduced FRC, basal atelectasis, V/Q mismatch [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Persist for many days post-operatively [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * V/Q mismatch causes hypoxemia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Atelectasis and reduced lung volume increase work of breathing [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.3.3. CONTRIBUTING FACTORS

* **Inadequate analgesia**:
  * Following laparotomy, sternotomy, thoracotomy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Results in inadequate cough [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Limited inspiration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Exacerbates atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Predisposes to secretion retention and pneumonia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Post-operative shivering**:
  * Occurs at emergence from anesthesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Increases metabolic demand [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Coincides with blunted respiratory center response from residual anesthetic effects [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * May result in type 1 or 2 respiratory failure [A:109-110]
* **Neuromuscular blockade issues**:
  * Inadequate reversal causing respiratory muscle weakness [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Exacerbated by electrolyte disturbances (hypokalaemia, hypocalcaemia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Related to fluid shifts and increased mineralocorticoid activity from stress response [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Post-operative pulmonary complications are common, particularly after thoracic and upper abdominal surgery, with contributing factors including inadequate analgesia limiting effective breathing and coughing, post-operative shivering increasing oxygen demand, and residual neuromuscular blockade affecting respiratory muscle function.

## 19.4. MINIMIZING RESPIRATORY COMPLICATIONS

**Strategies to reduce perioperative pulmonary complications.**

### 19.4.1. MULTIFACTORIAL APPROACH

* Addresses multiple factors discussed above [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* Comprehensive strategy needed [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

### 19.4.2. KEY COMPONENTS

* **Preoperative assessment**:
  * Risk stratification based on surgery type and underlying lung disease [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Anesthetic technique selection**:
  * Regional versus general anesthesia when feasible [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Regional techniques avoid many adverse effects of general anesthesia on lungs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Surgical approach**:
  * Laparoscopic surgery reduces post-operative pulmonary complications [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Intraoperative ventilation strategy**:
  * Low tidal volume ventilation (<8 mL/kg) significantly reduces post-operative pulmonary complications [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Optimal PEEP level and role of recruitment maneuvers not yet definitively established [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Oxygen management**:
  * Avoid 100% O2 to prevent absorption atelectasis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Even 20% nitrogen in fresh gas flow helps splint alveoli open [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Temperature management**:
  * Maintain normothermia to minimize risk of post-operative shivering [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Neuromuscular management**:
  * Proper monitoring and reversal of neuromuscular blockade [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Long-acting agents (e.g., pancuronium) more likely to cause post-operative respiratory failure than shorter-acting agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
* **Pain management**:
  * Adequate analgesia especially important after upper abdominal and thoracic surgery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]
  * Regional techniques (thoracic epidural, paravertebral catheter) often used for continuous local anesthetic infusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** A comprehensive approach to minimizing respiratory complications includes preoperative risk assessment, appropriate anesthetic technique selection, lung-protective ventilation strategies, avoidance of high FiO2, temperature management, thorough neuromuscular blockade reversal, and effective post-operative pain control, particularly for high-risk surgeries.
